[formula]
[formula]
[formula]

Figure 4.2
:
The two branches
W_{0}
and
W_{-1}
of Lambert’s W-function and the solutions
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
of the characteristic equation of the DDE (
4.3
).

4.3
Exponential Attraction of Special Solutions

Special solutions are an important concept of DDEs with small delay
\tau
, as they also characterize the behavior of general solutions of the DDE (
4.1
). Recall that a general solution
y:[t_{0}-\tau,t_{0}+c)
of the DDE (
4.1
) with
c\in(0,\infty]
and initial data
y_{t_{0}}(t)=u(t)
for
t\in[-\tau,0]
is denoted by
y(t_{0},u)
for
(t_{0},u)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{C}
. If Assumption
4.2
is fulfilled, then by Theorem
4.3
every solution of the DDE (
4.1
) exists on the time interval
[t_{0}-\tau,\infty)
. The following theorem shows that every solution of the DDE (
4.1
) is exponentially fast attracted to some special solution.

Theorem 4.7

(Exponential Attraction
[
27
]
)
.

Let
F\in C^{0,0}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{C},\mathbb{R}^{m})
fulfill Assumption
4.2
and assume
K\tau e<1
. Then for each initial data
(t_{0},u)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{C}
with solution
y(t_{0},u):[t_{0}-\tau,\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
of the DDE (
4.1
), there exists the limit

\bar{y}_{0}\coloneqq\lim_{s\rightarrow\infty}\bar{y}(s,y(t_{0},u)(s))(t_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.

(4.5)

The special solution
\bar{y}(t_{0},\bar{y}_{0})
of (
4.1
) through
(t_{0},\bar{y}_{0})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
, uniquely determined by the limit
\bar{y}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
corresponds to the given solution
y(t_{0},u)
and is denoted by

S(y(t_{0},u))=\bar{y}(t_{0},\bar{y}_{0}).

For the given solution and the corresponding special solution, it holds

C_{u}\coloneqq\sup_{t\geq t_{0}}e^{\frac{t}{\tau}}\left\lVert y(t_{0},u)(t)-S(y(t_{0},u))(t)\right\rVert_{\infty}<\infty.

For fixed
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, the upper bound
C_{u}
depends continuously on the initial data
u
.

The limit in Theorem
4.7
determines the initial condition
\bar{y}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
at time
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
of the special solution
\bar{y}
, such that in the limit
s\rightarrow\infty
, the special solution
\bar{y}(t_{0},y_{0})
and the given solution
y(t_{0},u)
coincide. The estimate of Theorem
4.7
guarantees that every solution is exponentially fast attracted towards a special solution
\bar{y}
. In the proof of our main theorem in Section
5.5
, we need the continuous dependence of
C_{u}
on
u
to find a common upper bound
C
for all initial conditions
u
in a given compact set.

A direct consequence of Theorem
4.7
is that all solutions are exponentially fast attracted to the invariant and finite-dimensional manifold of special solutions of the DDE (
4.1
). A manifold with this property is called an inertial manifold.

Corollary 4.8

(Inertial Manifold
[
4
]
)
.

The manifold of special solutions

\mathcal{M}\coloneqq\left\{\bar{y}_{t}:\bar{y}\textup{ is a special solution of the DDE \eqref{eq:DDEnonautonomous}}\right\}\subset\mathcal{C}

is an inertial manifold, i.e., it is invariant, finite-dimensional and exponentially attracts all solutions of (
4.1
).

In the following, we continue with the one-dimensional linear DDE with discrete delay of Example
4.6
, illustrate the exponential convergence of Theorem
4.7
, and explicitly determine the inertial manifold
\mathcal{M}
of Corollary
4.8
.

Example 4.9

(One-Dimensional Linear DDE continued)
.

Consider the one-dimensional linear DDE with discrete delay

\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}t}=F(t,y_{t})=K_{0}y(t-\tau)

(4.6)

with
F\in C^{0,\infty}_{b}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{C},\mathbb{R})
,
K_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, delay
\tau>0
and Lipschitz constant
K=\left\lvert K_{0}\right\rvert
, which fulfills Assumption
4.2
. If the smallness assumption
K\tau e<1
is fulfilled, the unique special solution with initial condition
y(t_{0})=y_{0}
is by Example
4.6
given by

y_{\lambda_{1}}(t_{0},y_{0})(t)=y_{0}e^{\lambda_{1}(t-t_{0})},

where
\lambda_{1}
is the unique solution of the characteristic equation
\lambda=K_{0}e^{-\lambda\tau}
with
\lambda_{1}>-\frac{1}{\tau}
. Consequently, the inertial manifold of special solutions is given by

\mathcal{M}\coloneqq\;\left\{\bar{y}_{t}:\bar{y}=y_{\lambda_{1}}(t_{0},y_{0}),y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}\right\}=\left\{y_{0}e^{\lambda_{1}(t+s-t_{0})}:y_{0}\in\mathbb{R},s\in[-\tau,0]\right\}\subset\mathcal{C}.

To illustrate the exponential attraction of Theorem
4.7
, we consider as in Example
4.6
the solution
y(t_{0},c_{y_{0}}):[t_{0}-\tau,\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
of (
4.6
) with constant initial data
c_{y_{0}}\in\mathcal{C}
. If
K\tau e<1
, then Theorem
4.7
implies that the limit

\bar{y}_{0}\coloneqq\lim_{s\rightarrow\infty}\bar{y}(s,y(t_{0},c_{y_{0}})(s))(t_{0})=\lim_{s\rightarrow\infty}y_{\lambda_{1}}(s,y(t_{0},c_{y_{0}})(s))(t_{0})=\lim_{s\rightarrow\infty}y(t_{0},c_{y_{0}})(s)e^{\lambda_{1}(t_{0}-s)}\in\mathbb{R}

exists. Hence, the special solution corresponding to
y(t_{0},c_{y_{0}})
is given by
S(y(t_{0},c_{y_{0}}))=\bar{y}(t_{0},\bar{y}_{0})
. Furthermore, there exists a tube of size
\pm C_{y_{0}}e^{-\frac{t}{\tau}}
around the special solution
\bar{y}(t_{0},\bar{y}_{0})
, in which the solution
y(t_{0},c_{y_{0}})
is contained for
t\geq t_{0}
, as shown in Figure
4.3
.

4.4
Special Solutions as Solutions of ODEs

In Section
4.2
, we have seen that it is sufficient to specify one initial condition
y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
at time
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
instead of an initial function
u\in\mathcal{C}
to define a unique special solution of the DDE (
4.1
). Consequently, the manifold
\mathcal{M}
of special solutions is, by Corollary
4.8
, a finite-dimensional inertial manifold. In the following theorem, we show that special solutions are not only solutions of DDEs, but there exists for every special solution
\bar{y}
a finite-dimensional initial value problem, which is uniquely solved by
\bar{y}
. To prove the upcoming theorem, we need a lemma estimating the distance between special solutions. The lemma uses the solution
\lambda_{1}
of the characteristic equation
\lambda=K_{0}e^{-\lambda\tau}
of the linear DDE introduced in Example
4.6
for the case
K_{0}<0
, so
K=-K_{0}
.

Lemma 4.10

(Exponential Growth Bound
[
12
]
)
.

Let
F\in C^{0,0}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{C},\mathbb{R}^{m})
fulfill Assumption
4.2
and assume
K\tau e<1
. Let
\lambda_{1}
be the unique solution of the characteristic equation
\lambda=-Ke^{-\lambda\tau}
with
-\frac{1}{\tau}<\lambda_{1}<0
. Then it holds
K<|\lambda_{1}|<Ke
and it follows for the special solutions
\bar{y}(t_{0},y_{0})
,
\bar{y}(t_{0},z_{0})
of the DDE (
4.1
) with
(t_{0},y_{0}),(t_{0},z_{0})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
that

\left\lVert\bar{y}(t_{0},y_{0})(t)-\bar{y}(t_{0},z_{0})(t)\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\begin{cases}\left\lVert y_{0}-z_{0}\right\rVert_{\infty}e^{|\lambda_{1}|(t_{0}-t)}&\text{if }t\leq t_{0},\\
\left\lVert y_{0}-z_{0}\right\rVert_{\infty}e^{|\lambda_{1}|\tau+K(t-t_{0})}&\text{if }t>t_{0}.\end{cases}

The following theorem defines for every special solution
\bar{y}
an initial value problem, which is uniquely solved by
\bar{y}
. Hereby, the vector field of the defined initial value problem only depends on the vector field
F
of the DDE (
4.1
), not on the specific special solution solving the initial value problem.

Theorem 4.11

(ODE for Special Solutions)
.

Let
F\in C^{0,0}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{C},\mathbb{R}^{m})
fulfill Assumption
4.2
and assume
K\tau e<1
. Then for every
(t_{0},y_{0})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
, the initial value problem

\frac{\mathrm{d}z(t)}{\mathrm{d}t}=\widetilde{F}(t,z(t))\coloneqq F(t,[\bar{y}(t,z(t))]_{t}),\qquad z(t_{0})=y_{0},

(4.7)

with
\widetilde{F}\in C^{0,0}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbb{R}^{m})
, is uniquely solved by the special solution
\bar{y}(t_{0},y_{0})
of the DDE (
4.1
). The vector field
\widetilde{F}
is globally Lipschitz continuous with respect to the second variable with Lipschitz constant
|\lambda_{1}|<Ke
, where
\lambda_{1}
is the unique solution of the characteristic equation
\lambda=-Ke^{-\lambda\tau}
with
-\frac{1}{\tau}<\lambda_{1}<0
.

[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]

Figure 4.3
:
Solution
y(t_{0},c_{y_{0}})
of the linear one-dimensional DDE (
4.6
) with constant initial data
c_{y_{0}}
and special solution
\bar{y}(t_{0},\bar{y}_{0})
through the point
(t_{0},\bar{y}_{0})
towards which the solution
y(t_{0},c_{y_{0}})
is exponentially attracted. The exponential tube around the special solution
\bar{y}(t_{0},\bar{y}_{0})
, which contains the general solution
y(t_{0},c_{y_{0}})
, is highlighted in green.

Proof.

The idea of the construction of the vector field
\widetilde{F}
is stated in
[
4
]
without proof. The given initial value problem is solved by the special solution
\bar{y}(t_{0},y_{0})
, as

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\;\bar{y}(t_{0},y_{0})(t)

\displaystyle=F(t,[\bar{y}(t_{0},y_{0})]_{t})

\displaystyle=F(t,[\bar{y}(t,\bar{y}(t_{0},y_{0})(t))]_{t})=\widetilde{F}(t,\bar{y}(t_{0},y_{0})(t)),

where we used in the first step, that
\bar{y}(t_{0},y_{0})
is a solution of the DDE (
4.1
) and in the second step that
\bar{y}(t_{0},y_{0})=\bar{y}(t,\bar{y}(t_{0},y_{0})(t))
because the unique special solution through the point
(t_{0},y_{0})
also passes through the point
(t,\bar{y}(t_{0},y_{0})(t))
. The last step follows from the definition of the vector field
\widetilde{F}
. The initial condition
z(t_{0})=y_{0}
is fulfilled, as
\bar{y}(t_{0},y_{0})(t_{0})=y_{0}
.

By Assumption
4.2
, the vector field
F
is globally Lipschitz continuous with respect to the second variable with Lipschitz constant
K
. Hence, it follows for the vector field
\widetilde{F}\in C^{0,0}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbb{R}^{m})
that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\widetilde{F}(t,z(t))-\widetilde{F}(t,y(t))\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert F(t,[\bar{y}(t,z(t))]_{t})-F(t,[\bar{y}(t,y(t))]_{t})\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq K\left\lVert[\bar{y}(t,z(t))]_{t}-[\bar{y}(t,y(t))]_{t}\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle=K\cdot\sup_{s\in[-\tau,0]}\left\lVert\bar{y}(t,z(t))(t+s)-\bar{y}(t,y(t))(t+s)\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq K\cdot\sup_{s\in[-\tau,0]}\left\lVert z(t)-y(t)\right\rVert_{\infty}e^{-|\lambda_{1}|s}

\displaystyle=Ke^{|\lambda_{1}|\tau}\left\lVert z(t)-y(t)\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle=|\lambda_{1}|\left\lVert z(t)-y(t)\right\rVert_{\infty}

for all
(t,y(t)),(t,z(t))\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Consequently,
\widetilde{F}
is globally Lipschitz continuous with respect to the second variable with Lipschitz constant
|\lambda_{1}|
. In the second line, we used the global Lipschitz continuity of
F
with respect to the second variable. In the fourth line, Lemma
4.10
is used to estimate the difference between the two special solutions. The last step follows from the property that
\lambda_{1}<0
solves
\lambda_{1}=-Ke^{-\lambda_{1}\tau}
, so
\left\lvert\lambda_{1}\right\rvert=Ke^{\left\lvert\lambda_{1}\right\rvert\tau}
.

As the vector field
F
is globally Lipschitz continuous with respect to the second variable, the Picard-Lindelöf Theorem (cf.
[
20
]
) implies that
\bar{y}(t_{0},y_{0})
is the unique solution of the given initial value problem.
∎

The last theorem implies that the dynamical system on the inertial manifold
\mathcal{M}
of special solutions generated by the DDE (
4.1
) agrees with the dynamical system generated by the ODE (
4.7
). For a fixed initial condition
(t_{0},y_{0})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
, the special solution
\bar{y}(t_{0},y_{0})
of the DDE (
4.1
) agrees with the unique solution of the initial value problem (
4.7
).

In the following, we study again the one-dimensional linear DDE with discrete delay of Examples
4.6
and
4.9
and determine the ODE (
4.7
) of Theorem
4.11
generating all special solutions.

Example 4.12

(One-Dimensional Linear DDE continued)
.

Consider the one-dimensional linear DDE with discrete delay

\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}t}=F(t,y_{t})=K_{0}y(t-\tau)

(4.8)

with
F\in C^{0,\infty}_{b}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{C},\mathbb{R})
,
K_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, delay
\tau>0
and Lipschitz constant
K=\left\lvert K_{0}\right\rvert
, which fulfills Assumption
4.2
. If the smallness assumption
K\tau e<1
is fulfilled, the unique special solution with initial condition
y(t_{0})=y_{0}
is by Example
4.6
given by

y_{\lambda_{1}}(t_{0},y_{0})(t)=y_{0}e^{\lambda_{1}(t-t_{0})},

where
\lambda_{1}
is the unique solution of the characteristic equation
\lambda=K_{0}e^{-\lambda\tau}
with
\lambda_{1}>-\frac{1}{\tau}
. Theorem
4.11
states that the special solution of the DDE (
4.8
) with initial data
(t_{0},y_{0})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
is the unique solution of the initial value problem

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{F}(t,y)\coloneqq F(t,[\bar{y}(t,y(t))]_{t})=K_{0}y(t)e^{-\lambda_{1}\tau}=\lambda_{1}y,

(4.9)

\displaystyle y(t_{0})

\displaystyle=y_{0},

where the characteristic equation
\lambda_{1}=K_{0}e^{-\lambda_{1}\tau}
was used.
Indeed, the unique,
\lambda_{1}
-dependent solution of the linear initial value problem (
4.9
) is the special solution
y_{\lambda_{1}}(t_{0},y_{0})
.

4.5
Neural DDEs with Small Delay

In this section, we aim to relate the results obtained in the last Sections
4.1
to
4.4
, to neural DDEs with the architecture of Definition
2.7
introduced in Section
2.2
. A neural DDE
\Phi\in\textup{NDDE}_{\tau}^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
,
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
open,
0\leq\tau\leq T
and
k\geq 0
is according to equation (
2.9
) defined by

\Phi:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{q},\quad x\mapsto\tilde{\lambda}(y(0,c_{\lambda(x)})(T))=\widetilde{W}\cdot y(0,c_{Wx+b})(T)+\tilde{b},

(4.10)

with two affine linear layers
\lambda
,
\tilde{\lambda}
and vector field
F:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{C}
satisfying Assumption
2.4
. The vector field
F
is defined on an open set
\Omega=\Omega_{t}\times\Omega_{y}\subset\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{C}
with
[0,T]\subset\Omega_{t}
. For general neural DDEs, it is sufficient if the vector field is defined on an open set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{C}
, but for the results of the last sections, it is necessary to study vector fields which are globally defined on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{C}
. In the following, we introduce a notation for all neural DDE architectures
\Phi\in\textup{NDDE}_{\tau}^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
,
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where the underlying vector field is globally defined and fulfills Assumption
4.2
.

Definition 4.13

(Weakly Nonlinear Neural DDE)
.

The subset of all neural DDE architectures
\Phi\in\textup{NDDE}_{\tau}^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
,
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
open,
k\geq 0
, for which the underlying vector field
F
is globally defined and weakly nonlinear, i.e., it fulfills Assumption
4.2
, is denoted by
\overline{\textup{NDDE}}^{k}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})\subset C^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
.

In the following we show that for any neural DDE
\Phi\in\textup{NDDE}_{\tau}^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
,
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
open,
k\geq 1
, with vector field
F:\Omega_{t}\times\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
k\geq 1
and
[0,T]\subset\Omega_{t}
, there exists a globally defined, weakly nonlinear vector field
\overline{F}\in C_{b}^{0,k}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{C},\mathbb{R}^{m})
corresponding to a neural DDE
\overline{\Phi}\in\overline{\textup{NDDE}}^{k}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
, such that
\Phi(x)=\overline{\Phi}(x)
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
. For general neural DDEs
\Phi\in\textup{NDDE}_{\tau}^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
,
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
open,
k\geq 1
, it holds for the underlying vector field that
F\in C_{b}^{0,k}(\Omega_{t}\times\Omega_{y},\mathbb{R}^{m})
,
k\geq 1
, where
\Omega_{t}\times\Omega_{y}\subset\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{C}
open. The following theorem makes the additional assumption on the vector field
F
that
\Omega_{y}=\mathcal{C}
, i.e., the vector field is globally defined with respect to the second variable.

Theorem 4.14

(Equivalent Weakly Nonlinear Neural DDE)
.

Let
\Phi\in\textup{NDDE}_{\tau}^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
,
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
open,
k\geq 1
, with vector field
F:\Omega_{t}\times\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
with global Lipschitz constant
K
with respect to the second variable. Then there exists a neural DDE
\overline{\Phi}\in\overline{\textup{NDDE}}_{\tau}^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
, such that
\Phi(x)=\overline{\Phi}(x)
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
, which is based on a vector field
\overline{F}\in C_{b}^{0,k}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{C},\mathbb{R}^{m})
with global Lipschitz constant
K
with respect to the second variable and

\overline{F}\big|_{[0,T]\times\mathcal{C}}=F\big|_{[0,T]\times\mathcal{C}}.

Proof.

Let
\Phi\in\textup{NDDE}_{\tau}^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
,
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
open,
k\geq 1
, with vector field
F:\Omega_{t}\times\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
. By Definition
2.7
, it holds
F\in C_{b}^{0,k}(\Omega_{t}\times\mathcal{C},\mathbb{R}^{m})
,
k\geq 1
and Assumption
2.4
implies
[0,T]\subset\Omega_{t}
with
\Omega_{t}\subset\mathbb{R}
open. Hence, the restricted vector field
\widetilde{F}\coloneqq F|_{[0,T]\times\mathcal{C}}
fulfills
\widetilde{F}\in C_{b}^{0,k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{C},\mathbb{R}^{m})
. We define the globally extended vector field

\overline{F}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m},\qquad\overline{F}(t,y_{t})\coloneqq\begin{cases}\widetilde{F}(0,y_{t})&\text{ if }t<0,\\
\widetilde{F}(t,y_{t})&\text{ if }0\leq t\leq T,\\
\widetilde{F}(T,y_{t})&\text{ if }t>T.\end{cases}

It holds
\overline{F}\in C_{b}^{0,k}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{m})
, as by construction
\overline{F}
is continuous in time and as
\widetilde{F}\in C_{b}^{0,k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{C},\mathbb{R}^{m})
, also
\overline{F}
has in the second variable bounded derivatives up to order
k
for every fixed
t
.
As
F
is globally Lipschitz continuous with respect to the second variable on
\Omega_{t}\times\mathcal{C}
, the vector field
\overline{F}
is globally Lipschitz continuous on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{C}
with respect to the second variable with the same Lipschitz constant
K
, as

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left\lVert\overline{F}(t,y_{t})-\overline{F}(t,z_{t})\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\lVert\widetilde{F}(t,y_{t})-\widetilde{F}(t,z_{t})\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\lVert F(t,y_{t})-F(t,z_{t})\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq K\left\lVert y_{t}-z_{t}\right\rVert_{\infty}\qquad\text{for all }y_{t},z_{t}\in\mathcal{C}.

The constructed vector field
\overline{F}
fulfills Assumption
4.2
:
\overline{F}
is continuous and globally defined and we can estimate

\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left\lVert\overline{F}(t,0)\right\rVert_{\infty}=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\lVert\overline{F}(t,0)\right\rVert_{\infty}\eqqcolon A<\infty

for some constant
A\geq 0
, as
t\mapsto\left\lVert\overline{F}(t,0)\right\rVert_{\infty}
is a continuous function, which attains its maximum on the compact interval
[0,T]
. Furthermore,
\overline{F}
is globally Lipschitz continuous with respect to the second variable, where the global bound of the first derivative with respect to the second variable can be taken as a global Lipschitz constant. Consequently, the DDE

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle=\overline{F}(t,y_{t})\qquad

\displaystyle\text{for }t\geq t_{0},

\displaystyle y_{t_{0}}(t)

\displaystyle=u(t)

\displaystyle\text{for }t\in[-\tau,0],

has by Theorem
4.3
for every initial data
(t_{0},u)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{C}
a unique, continuous and globally defined solution
y(t_{0},u):[t_{0}-\tau,\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

The vector field
\overline{F}
also fulfills Assumption
2.4
: it holds
[0,T]\subset\mathbb{R}
and
[-\tau,T]\subset\mathcal{I}_{y_{0}}
, as all solutions of the DDE with initial data
(0,c_{y_{0}})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{C}
exist globally on the time interval
[-\tau,\infty)
. Furthermore, it holds in the notation of Assumption
2.4
that
\Omega_{0}\subset\Omega_{y}=\mathcal{C}
. Hence,
\overline{\Phi}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{q}
,
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
open, defined by (
4.10
) with vector field
\overline{F}\in C_{b}^{0,k}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{C},\mathbb{R}^{m})
,
k\geq 1
, is by Definition
2.7
a neural DDE
\overline{\Phi}\in\textup{NDDE}_{\tau}^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
. Definition
4.13
implies, that it holds especially
\overline{\Phi}\in\overline{\textup{NDDE}}_{\tau}^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
. As by construction of the vector field
\overline{F}
it holds

\overline{F}\big|_{[0,T]\times\mathcal{C}}=\widetilde{F}\big|_{[0,T]\times\mathcal{C}}=F\big|_{[0,T]\times\mathcal{C}},

it follows that
\Phi(x)=\overline{\Phi}(x)
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
.
∎

Remark 4.15
.

The proof of Theorem
4.14
even shows a stronger statement: it is not necessary to assume
\Phi\in\textup{NDDE}_{\tau}^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
,
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
open,
k\geq 1
, which includes checking that the underlying vector field fulfills Assumption
2.4
. If a map
\Phi
is defined by (
4.10
) with vector field
F\in C_{b}^{0,k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{C},\mathbb{R}^{m})
,
k\geq 1
, then the extended vector field
\overline{F}
automatically fulfills Assumptions
2.4
and
4.2
.

5
Approximation of Local Extreme Points

In this section, we formulate and prove our main result, Theorem
5.4
, about the non-universal approximation property of non-augmented neural DDEs with sufficiently small memory capacity
K\tau
. Under certain assumptions on the Lipschitz constant
K
and the delay
\tau
of the neural DDE, we show that for functions
\Psi
, which have in one component a non-degenerate local extreme point, there exists no neural DDE approximating
\Psi
with accuracy
\varepsilon
in the sup-norm. As such, a function
\Psi
can be chosen smooth; it follows directly that non-augmented neural DDEs cannot have the universal approximation property with respect to the space
C^{j}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})
for every
j\geq 0
, as stated in the upcoming Corollary
5.5
. In Section
3.3
, we already introduced Theorem
3.13
, which is a direct consequence of the results shown in this section.

To prove our main result, we introduce in Section
5.1
functions with local non-degenerate extreme points. These functions can be characterized via Morse functions, which are functions where every critical point is non-degenerate. For our main result, we are interested in those critical points that are either a local minimum or a local maximum. In Section
5.2
, we state our main Theorem
5.4
and its Corollary
5.5
. In preparation to prove our main result, we study in Section
5.3
the initial behavior of the neural DDE, given by the first affine linear transformation and the dynamics of the underlying DDE itself under the assumption that the memory capacity
K\tau
is sufficiently small. Afterwards, we study in Section
5.4
necessary conditions for an approximation of
\Psi
by a neural DDE and geometrically interpret these conditions as a separation of the phase space by level sets. Finally, we are able to prove in Section
5.5
our main Theorem
5.4
.

5.1
Characterization of Non-Degenerate Extreme Points

A twice continuously scalar differentiable function
\Psi\in C^{2}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R})
,
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
open, has a critical point
p\in\mathcal{X}
if its gradient vanishes, i.e.,
\nabla\Psi(p)=0
. Depending on the rank of the Hessian matrix of
\Psi
at the point
p
, the critical point is called degenerate or non-degenerate. Functions, where every critical point is non-degenerate, are called Morse functions, as defined in the following.

Definition 5.1

(Morse Function
[
22
,
39
]
)
.

A map
\Psi\in C^{2}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R})
with
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
open is called a Morse function if all critical points of
\Psi
are non-degenerate, i.e., for every critical point
p\in\mathcal{X}
defined by a zero gradient
\nabla\Psi(p)=0\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the Hessian matrix
H_{\Psi}(p)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is non-singular. A critical point
p\in\mathcal{X}
of a Morse function has index
r
, if
r
eigenvalues of
H_{\Psi}(p)
are negative.

It is of interest to study Morse functions, as they are dense in the Banach space of
k
times continuously differentiable functions from
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to
\mathbb{R}
, if
k\geq n+1\geq 2
, cf.
[
33
]
. Hence, it is a generic property of a sufficiently smooth function to be a Morse function. The following lemma shows that every Morse function can be locally transformed into a quadratic form.

Lemma 5.2

(Morse-Palais Lemma
[
22
,
40
]
)
.

Let
\Psi\in C^{k+2}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R})
with
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
open,
k\geq 1
be a Morse function with critical point
p\in\mathcal{X}
with index
r
. Then there exists a neighborhood
\mathcal{U}
of
0\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and a
C^{k}
-diffeomorphism
\mu:\mathcal{U}\rightarrow\mu(\mathcal{U})\subset\mathcal{X}
with
\mu(0)=p
, such that for
(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n})\in\mathcal{U}

\Psi(\mu(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}))=\Psi(p)-\sum_{j=1}^{r}u_{j}^{2}+\sum_{j=r+1}^{n}u_{j}^{2}.

(5.1)

In the case that the index
r
is zero, the map
\Psi\circ\mu:\mathcal{U}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
has a local minimum at the critical point
0\in\mathcal{U}
, in the case that
r=n
, the map
\Psi\circ\mu
has a local maximum at
0\in\mathcal{U}
. Our main Theorem
5.4
is based on non-degenerate local extreme points, i.e., Morse functions with a critical point with index
0
or
n
. Hence, our results apply to all functions
\Psi
, which have somewhere in their domain of definition a local non-degenerate extreme point.

In order to restrict our analysis to the open neighborhood
\mathcal{U}
, we define in the following the radius of definition
r_{0}
of a critical point of a map
\Psi
of Lemma
5.2
. To use the symmetry properties of (
5.1
), we first define the open and closed Euclidean balls, given for
s>0
and
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
by

\displaystyle B_{s}(v)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\;\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}-v_{i})^{2}<s^{2}\right\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n},

\displaystyle K_{s}(v)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\;\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}-v_{i})^{2}\leq s^{2}\right\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}.

By definition, the boundary and interior of the open and closed balls
B_{s}(v)
and
K_{s}(v)
are given by

\partial K_{s}(v)=\partial B_{s}(v)=K_{s}(v)\setminus B_{s}(v),\qquad\text{int}(K_{s}(v))=\text{int}(B_{s}(v))=B_{s}(v).

In the following, we can now define the radius of definition
r_{0}>0
of a critical point of a function
\Psi
of Lemma
5.2
.

Definition 5.3

(Radius of Definition)
.

Assume the setting of Lemma
5.2
. Every
r_{0}>0
with
K_{r_{0}}(0)\subset\mathcal{U}
is called a radius of definition of the critical point
p\in\mathcal{X}
of the Morse function
\Psi
.

Given a radius of definition
r_{0}>0
of a critical point of a function
\Psi
, the map
\Psi\circ\mu:K_{r_{0}}(0)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
attains in the case of an index
r=0
(
r=n
) its global maximum (minimum) at the boundary
\partial K_{r_{0}}(0)
and its global minimum (maximum) at
0\in K_{r_{0}}(0)
. The main idea of the upcoming proof is the property, that in order to approximate the map
\Psi
, a neural DDE has to linearly separate the inner point
0\in K_{r_{0}}(0)
from every point of boundary
\partial K_{r_{0}}(0)
. If the index of the critical point is between
1
and
n-1
, the map
\Psi\circ\mu:\mathcal{U}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
has a saddle point at
0\in\mathcal{U}
. As our proof is based on the property that
\Psi\circ\mu
has a constant value at the boundary
\partial K_{r_{0}}(0)
, our results only apply to local non-degenerate extreme points and not to saddle points.

5.2
Statement of the Main Result

In this section, we aim to study the universal approximation property of non-augmented neural DDEs in the sense of Definition
3.1
. A neural DDE as defined in (
2.9
) is a map
\Phi:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{q}
,
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
open, which should approximate the map
\Psi:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{q}
. For a given
\varepsilon>0
, the neural DDE
\Phi
approximates a map
\Psi
with accuracy
\varepsilon
, if

\left\lVert\Phi(x)-\Psi(x)\right\rVert_{\infty}<\varepsilon\qquad\forall\;x\in\mathcal{X}.

(5.2)

If the neural DDE is augmented, Theorem
3.14
guarantees, under certain assumptions, the universal embedding property with respect to the space of Lipschitz continuous functions, and hence also the universal approximation property. In the non-augmented case, we show via functions
\Psi
, which have in at least one component map
\Psi_{i}
a local non-degenerate extreme point, that for a sufficiently small memory capacity
K\tau
, no universal approximation is possible.

Theorem 5.4

(No Approximation of Non-Degenerate Local Extreme Points)
.

Let
\Psi:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{q}
,
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
open, have a component map
\Psi_{i}\in C^{3}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R})
, which has a non-degenerate local extreme point with radius of definition
r_{0}>0
and let
\varepsilon>0
with
2\varepsilon<r_{0}^{2}
. Furthermore, fix constants
K,w,\tilde{w}\in(0,\infty)
,
A\geq 0
and
k\geq 1
.

Then there exists a continuous function
\tau_{0}\in C^{0}((0,\infty),[0,T])
with
K\tau_{0}(K)e<1
for
K\in(0,\infty)
with the following property: if
\tau\in[0,\tau_{0}(K))
, every neural DDE
\Phi\in\textup{NDDE}_{\tau,\textup{N},K}^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
with

•

vector field
F:\Omega_{t}\times\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
with global Lipschitz constant
K
in its second variable on
\Omega_{t}\times\mathcal{C}
and
\left\lVert F(t,0)\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq A
for all
t\in[0,T]
,

•

weight matrices
W,\widetilde{W}
with
\big\lVert W\big\lVert_{\infty}\leq w
and
\big\lVert\widetilde{W}\big\lVert_{\infty}\leq\tilde{w}
,

cannot approximate the map
\Psi
with accuracy
\varepsilon
, i.e., for every neural DDE
\Phi\in\textup{NDDE}_{\tau,\textup{N},K}^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
, there exists a point
x\in\mathcal{X}
, such that

\left\lVert\Phi(x)-\Psi(x)\right\rVert_{\infty}\geq\varepsilon.

The theorem is proven in the upcoming Section
5.5
. In the setting of Theorem
5.4
, we assume that the underlying DDE of the considered neural DDE
\Phi\in\textup{NDDE}_{\tau,\textup{N},K}^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
uses constant initial data. With slight modifications, the proof also works for fixed, continuous, non-constant initial data, but the main idea stays the same. To maintain the analogy to DenseResNets as introduced in Section
2.1
, we focus on DDEs with constant initial data.

It is possible to show that there exist component maps
\Psi_{i}\in C^{3}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R})
with saddle points instead of non-degenerate local extreme points, such that under the assumptions of Theorem
5.4
, it is possible to find a neural DDE
\Phi\in\textup{NDDE}_{\tau,\textup{N},K}^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
,
k\geq 1
approximating
\Psi_{i}
in the
i
-th component. Hence, it is crucial to study local extreme points, as they have other topological properties than saddle points. A direct consequence of Theorem
5.4
is that for a sufficiently small memory capacity
K\tau
, non-augmented neural DDEs, and as a special case also non-augmented neural ODEs with
\tau=0
, cannot have the universal approximation property.

Corollary 5.5

(No Universal Approximation for Non-Augmented Neural DDEs with Small Memory Capacity)
.

There exists a continuous function
\tau_{0}\in C^{0}((0,\infty),[0,T])
with
K\tau_{0}(K)e<1
for
K\in(0,\infty)
with the following property: if
\tau\in[0,\tau_{0}(K)]
and constants
K,w,\tilde{w}\in(0,\infty)
,
A\geq 0
, are fixed, the class of neural DDEs
\textup{NDDE}_{\tau,\textup{N},K}^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
,
k\geq 1
,
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
with

•

vector field
F:\Omega_{t}\times\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
with global Lipschitz constant
K
in its second variable on
\Omega_{t}\times\mathcal{C}
and
\left\lVert F(t,0)\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq A
for all
t\in[0,T]
,

•

weight matrices
W,\widetilde{W}
with
\big\lVert W\big\lVert_{\infty}\leq w
and
\big\lVert\widetilde{W}\big\lVert_{\infty}\leq\tilde{w}
,

does not have the universal approximation property with respect to the space
C^{j}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
for every
j\geq 0
.

Proof.

By Definition
3.1
, the class of neural DDEs
\textup{NDDE}_{\tau,\textup{N},K}^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
does not have the universal approximation property, if there exists some
\varepsilon>0
and for a given
j\geq 0
a map
\Psi\in C^{j}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
, such that for every choice of the parameters of
\textup{NDDE}_{\tau,\textup{N},K}^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
, i.e., all possible weights and biases
W
,
\widetilde{W}
,
b
and
\tilde{b}
in the parameter space
\mathbb{V}
and vector field
F
of the DDE, there exists a point
x^{\ast}\in\mathcal{X}
, such that

\left\lVert\Phi(x^{\ast})-\Psi(x^{\ast})\right\rVert_{\infty}\geq\varepsilon.

Let
\Psi:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{q}
,
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
open,
x^{\ast}\in\text{int}(\mathcal{X})
with a component map
\Psi_{i}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R})
,
\Psi_{i}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}-x_{i}^{\ast})^{2}
. By choosing the other components of
\Psi
sufficiently smooth, we can assume that
\Psi\in C^{j}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
for a given
j\geq 0
. Let
r_{0}>0
, such that
K_{r_{0}}(x^{\ast})\subset\mathcal{X}
and let
0<2\varepsilon<r_{0}^{2}
. Then with the identity transformation
\mu=\text{id}_{\mathcal{X}}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
as a
C^{\infty}
-diffeomorphism, the map
\Psi_{i}
has at
x^{\ast}
a local non-degenerate minimum with radius of definition
r_{0}>0
. Fix some constants
K,w,\tilde{w}\in(0,\infty)
, then it follows from Theorem
5.4
that under the given assumptions, any neural DDE does not have the universal approximation property with respect to the space
C^{j}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
for every
j\geq 0
.
∎

In the following Sections
5.3
and
5.4
, we prove several preparational lemmas to show Theorem
5.4
in Section
5.5
. In order to use the results about neural DDEs with small delay introduced in Section
4
, we study neural DDEs
\overline{\Phi}\in\overline{\textup{NDDE}}_{\tau,\textup{N},K}^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
, which are based on globally defined vector fields
\overline{F}\in C_{b}^{0,k}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{C},\mathbb{R}^{m})
, which fulfill Assumption
4.2
with constants
A\geq 0
and
K>0
. The dynamics of the neural DDE
\overline{\Phi}
can be subdivided in three maps:

\overline{\Phi}=\tilde{\lambda}\circ H\circ\lambda,

where
\tilde{\lambda}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{q}
and
\lambda:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
are the affine linear maps before and after the DDE, as defined in (
2.9
). The solution map of the underlying DDE

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle=\overline{F}(t,y_{t})\qquad

\displaystyle\text{for }t\geq 0,

(5.3)

\displaystyle y(t)

\displaystyle=c_{y_{1}}(t)

\displaystyle\text{for }t\in[-\tau,0],

with constant initial data
c_{y_{1}}:[-\tau,0]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
c_{y_{1}}(t)=y_{1}
on the time interval
[0,T]
is denoted by

H:\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m},\qquad H(y_{1})=y(0,c_{y_{1}})(T).

(5.4)

The dynamics of the DDE (
5.3
) is subdivided in two intervals
[0,t^{\ast}]
and
[t^{\ast},T]
for some
t^{\ast}\in[0,T)
. The time
t^{\ast}
is parameterized in terms of the delay
\tau\geq 0
as
t^{\ast}=\beta\tau
with
\beta>0
. Given
\beta>0
, we need to choose
\tau<\frac{T}{\beta}
to guarantee
t^{\ast}<T
. The map
G:\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
is defined as the solution map of the DDE (
5.3
) with constant initial data
(0,c_{y_{1}})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{C}
on the time interval
[0,t^{\ast}]
:

G:\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m},\qquad G(y_{1})=y(0,c_{y_{1}})(t^{\ast}).

(5.5)

As the underlying DDE (
5.3
) is based on a vector field
\overline{F}
, which is globally Lipschitz continuous with respect to the second variable, the solution
y(0,c_{y_{1}})(t)
depends by Theorem
4.1
continuously on the initial data
c_{y_{1}}\in\mathcal{C}
and hence also continuously on
y_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Consequently, the solution maps
G
and
H
are continuous.

In the upcoming analysis, we use the subscript
0
for sets in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, before the affine linear map
\lambda
is applied. The main analysis is carried out on a compact set
\mathcal{K}_{0}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The subscript
1
is used for all sets in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, after the application of the affine linear map
\lambda
corresponding to the time
t=0
of the DDE (
5.3
), such as the compact set
\mathcal{K}_{1}\coloneqq\lambda(\mathcal{K}_{0})
. The subscript
2
is used for all sets in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
after the application of the map
G
, corresponding to the time
t=t^{\ast}
, such as the compact set
\mathcal{K}_{2}\coloneqq G(\mathcal{K}_{1}
). All sets in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, which correspond to the time
t=T
after the map
H
is applied, are denoted with a subscript
3
, such as the compact set
\mathcal{K}_{3}\coloneqq H(\mathcal{K}_{1})
. The output of the neural DDE after the application of the linear transformation
\tilde{\lambda}
is denoted with a subscript 4. The subscript notation and the maps
G
and
H
introduced in (
5.4
) and (
5.5
) are visualized in Figure
5.1
.
