Visualization of the subscript notation and the maps
G
and
H
introduced in this section. Additionally, the corresponding time intervals of the maps
\overline{G}
,
\overline{H}
, and
\overline{I}
introduced in the next section are shown.

5.3
Initial Behavior and DDE Dynamics

In this section, we study the initial behavior and the DDE dynamics of non-augmented neural DDEs, i.e., the dynamics of the first affine linear transformation
\lambda:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
and the solution maps
G
and
H
of the underlying DDE defined in (
5.4
) and (
5.5
).

For non-augmented neural DDEs, it holds
n\geq m
, i.e., the input dimension
n
is at least as large as the dimension
m
of the phase space of the underlying DDE. In this section, we restrict our analysis to the initial behavior of the neural DDE in the case that
n=m
; the case
n>m
is treated separately in the proof of Theorem
5.4
in Section
5.5
. An additional assumption we make on the map
\Psi
approximated by the neural DDE is that
\Psi
has a component map
\Psi_{i}
with a non-degenerate local minimum as an extreme point. The opposite case, that the non-degenerate local extreme point of
\Psi_{i}
is a local maximum, is also postponed to the proof in Section
5.5
.

In the following lemma, we study the behavior of the map
\Psi
, which has a component map
\Psi_{i}\in C^{3}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R})
with a non-degenerate local minimum, under the first affine linear layer
\lambda
and the assumption that
n=m
.

Lemma 5.6

(Initial Behavior under the Affine Linear Map
\lambda
)
.

Let
\Psi:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{q}
,
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
open, have a component map
\Psi_{i}\in C^{3}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R})
, which has a non-degenerate local minimum
p\in\mathcal{X}
with radius of definition
r_{0}>0
, i.e.,
\Psi_{i}
has on a neighborhood
\mathcal{U}
of
0\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
under the
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism
\mu:\mathcal{U}\rightarrow\mu(\mathcal{U})
with
\mu(0)=p
the form (
5.1
) and
K_{r_{0}}(0)\subset\mathcal{U}
. Let
\mathcal{K}_{0}\coloneqq\mu(K_{r_{0}}(0))
and
\lambda:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\lambda(x)=Wx+b
,
W\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
,
b\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an affine linear map with
\big\lVert W\big\lVert_{\infty}\leq w
for some constant
w>0
. Then the following assertions hold:

(a)

The map
\Psi_{i}:\mathcal{K}_{0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
attains its global minimum at
p
with value
\Psi_{i}(p)
and its global maximum at every point of the boundary
\partial\mathcal{K}_{0}
with value
\Psi_{i}(p)+r_{0}^{2}
.

(b)

If
\textup{rank}(W)=n
, there exists
r_{1}>0
, only depending on
w
and hence uniform for all weight matrices
W
with
\big\lVert W\big\lVert_{\infty}\leq w
, such that
K_{r_{1}}(\lambda(p))\subset\mathcal{K}_{1}
, where
\mathcal{K}_{1}\coloneqq\lambda(\mathcal{K}_{0})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is compact. The map
\Psi_{i}\circ\lambda^{-1}:\mathcal{K}_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
attains its global minimum at
\lambda(p)
with value
\Psi_{i}(p)
and its global maximum at every point of the boundary
\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
with value
\Psi_{i}(p)+r_{0}^{2}
.

(c)

If
\textup{rank}(W)<n
, then there exists
s\in\partial\mathcal{K}_{0}
with
\lambda(s)=\lambda(p)
.

Proof.

As
\Psi:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{q}
,
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
open, has a component map
\Psi_{i}\in C^{3}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R})
, which has a non-degenerate local minimum
p\in\mathcal{X}
with radius of definition
r_{0}>0
, there exists a
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism
\mu:\mathcal{U}\rightarrow\mu(\mathcal{U})
with
\mu(0)=p
and
K_{r_{0}}\subset\mathcal{U}
, such that for
(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n})\in\mathcal{U}

\Psi_{i}(\mu(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}))=\Psi_{i}(p)+\sum_{j=1}^{n}u_{j}^{2},

(5.6)

as a non-degenerate local minimum is a critical point with index
0
.

As the diffeomorphism
\mu
is continuous, the set
\mathcal{K}_{0}\coloneqq\mu(K_{r_{0}}(0))
is compact. It follows directly from (
5.6
) that the component map
\Psi_{i}:\mathcal{K}_{0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
attains its global minimum at
p=\mu(0)
with value
\Psi_{i}(p)
and its global maximum on all points of the boundary
\partial\mathcal{K}_{0}
with value
\Psi_{i}(p)+r_{0}^{2}
, as the boundary of
K_{r_{0}}
is mapped to the boundary of
\mathcal{K}_{0}
under the diffeomorphism
\mu
, which proves part (a).

If
\text{rank}(W)=n
, the affine linear map
\lambda:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\lambda(x)=Wx+b
is a diffeomorphism, which maps the compact set
\mathcal{K}_{0}
to the compact set
\mathcal{K}_{1}\coloneqq\lambda(\mathcal{K}_{0})
and the boundary
\partial\mathcal{K}_{0}
to the boundary
\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
. Consequently, the map
\Psi_{i}\circ\lambda^{-1}:\mathcal{K}_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
attains its global minimum at
\lambda(p)
with value
\Psi_{i}(p)
and its global maximum at all points of the boundary
\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
with value
\Psi_{i}(p)+r_{0}^{2}
. As
\mu
and
\lambda
are diffeomorphisms,
\mathcal{K}_{1}=\lambda(\mu(K_{r_{0}})(0))
is a full-dimensional subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and homeomorphic to the closed ball
K_{r_{0}}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
. As
0\in\text{int}(K_{r_{0}}(0))
, it follows that
p=\mu(0)\in\mu(\text{int}(K_{r_{0}}(0)))=\text{int}(\mathcal{K}_{0})
and that
\lambda(p)\in\mu(\text{int}(\mathcal{K}_{0}))=\text{int}(\mathcal{K}_{1})
. Consequently, there exists a closed ball around
\lambda(p)
with small enough radius, which is contained in
\mathcal{K}_{1}
, i.e., there exists a radius
r_{1}>0
, such that
K_{r_{1}}(\lambda(p))\subset\mathcal{K}_{1}
, which proves part (b). The radius
r_{1}
cannot get arbitrarily large, as the considered component map
\Psi_{i}
is fixed and the weight matrix
W
is bounded by
\big\lVert W\big\lVert_{\infty}\leq w
.

If
\text{rank}(W)<n
, then the kernel of the matrix
W

\text{ker}(W)=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:Wx=0\}

is non-empty. Let
x^{\ast}\in\text{ker}(W)
, then there exists an intersection of the line
p+\alpha x^{\ast}
,
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
with
\partial\mathcal{K}_{0}
, as
\mathcal{K}_{0}
is a full-dimensional subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
homeomorphic to the closed ball
K_{r_{0}}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
p\in\text{int}(\mathcal{K}_{0})
. Let

s\in\{p+\alpha x^{\ast}:\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\}\cap\partial\mathcal{K}_{0}

be such an intersecting point, then
s\in\partial\mathcal{K}_{0}
and
s=p+\alpha^{\ast}x^{\ast}
for some
\alpha^{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}
. By construction, it holds

\lambda(s)=\lambda(p+\alpha^{\ast}x^{\ast})=W(p+\alpha^{\ast}x^{\ast})+b=Wp+\alpha^{\ast}Wx^{\ast}+b=\lambda(p),

which proves part (c).
∎

In the following lemma, we study the initial behavior of the neural DDE
\overline{\Phi}
, i.e., the map
G
defined in (
5.5
) as the solution map of the DDE (
5.3
) on the time interval
[0,t^{\ast}]
. Hereby, we use the property that the time
t^{\ast}=\beta\tau
is sufficiently small, if the parameter
\beta
is fixed and the delay
\tau
is sufficiently small.

Lemma 5.7

(Initial Behavior under the Solution Map
G
)
.

Let the following assumptions be satisfied:

•

\mathcal{K}_{1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
is a compact set with
M\coloneqq\sup_{y_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}}\left\lVert y_{1}\right\rVert_{\infty}>0
and
\lambda(p)\in\mathcal{K}_{1}
with
K_{r_{1}}(\lambda(p))\subset\mathcal{K}_{1}
for some
r_{1}>0
.

•

The underlying vector field
\overline{F}
of the neural DDE
\overline{\Phi}\in\overline{\textup{NDDE}}^{k}_{\tau,\textup{N},K}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
,
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
open,
k\geq 0
, fulfills Assumption
4.2
with
A\geq 0
and
K>0
, which define the constant
C_{1}\coloneqq(KM+A)e^{KT}
.

•

The map
G:\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
is the solution map of the DDE (
5.3
) on the time interval
[0,t^{\ast}]
as defined in (
5.5
). It holds
t^{\ast}=\beta\tau
with
\beta>0
and
\delta_{1,t^{\ast}}\coloneqq C_{1}t^{\ast}
.

Then for every
\beta>0
and every
0<\kappa\leq r_{1}
, there exists an upper bound
\tau_{1,\beta,\kappa}=\frac{\kappa}{2C_{1}\beta}>0
, such that for all
\tau\in[0,\tau_{1,\beta,\kappa}]
and every
y_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}
it holds
G(y_{1})\in\mathcal{K}_{2}\coloneqq G(\mathcal{K}_{1})
and

\left\lVert G(y_{1})-y_{1}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\delta_{1,t^{\ast}}\leq\frac{\kappa}{2}.

Especially for
\lambda(p)\in\mathcal{K}_{1}
we have

G(\lambda(p))\in K_{\delta_{1,t^{\ast}}}(\lambda(p))\subset K_{\kappa/2}(\lambda(p))\subset K_{r_{1}}(\lambda(p))\subset\mathcal{K}_{1}.

Proof.

For
\beta>0
and
0<\kappa\leq r_{1}
, let
\tau_{1,\beta,\kappa}\coloneqq\frac{\kappa}{2C_{1}\beta}>0
, then it holds for all
\tau\in[0,\tau_{1,\beta,\kappa}]
that

2\delta_{1,t^{\ast}}=2C_{1}\beta\tau\leq 2C_{1}\beta\tau_{1,\beta,\kappa}=\kappa\leq r_{1}.

For
y_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}
we estimate

\displaystyle\left\lVert G(y_{1})-y_{1}\right\rVert_{\infty}=\left\lVert y(0,c_{y_{1}})(t^{\ast})-y(0,c_{y_{1}})(0)\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\left\lVert\int_{0}^{t^{\ast}}\overline{F}(t,y_{t})\;\mathrm{d}t\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\int_{0}^{t^{\ast}}\left\lVert\overline{F}(t,y_{t})-\overline{F}(t,0)\right\rVert_{\infty}+\left\lVert\overline{F}(t,0)\right\rVert_{\infty}\;\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t^{\ast}}(K\left\lVert y_{t}\right\rVert_{\infty}+A)\;\mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{0}^{t^{\ast}}\left(K\left(Me^{Kt}+\frac{A}{K}\left(e^{Kt}-1\right)\right)+A\right)\;\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t^{\ast}}(KM+A)e^{Kt}\;\mathrm{d}t\leq(KM+A)e^{KT}t^{\ast}=C_{1}t^{\ast}=C_{1}\beta\tau=\delta_{1,t^{\ast}}.

Hereby we first used the integral formulation of the DDE (
5.3
), then the Lipschitz continuity of
\overline{F}
and finally Theorem
4.3
(a) to estimate
\left\lVert y_{t}\right\rVert_{\infty}
with initial data
c_{y_{1}}
, which fulfills
\left\lVert c_{y_{1}}\right\rVert_{\infty}=\left\lVert y_{1}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq M
. Consequently, under the map
G
points move at most the distance
\delta_{1,t^{\ast}}
, such that

G(\lambda(p))\in K_{\delta_{1,t^{\ast}}}(\lambda(p))\subset K_{\kappa/2}(\lambda(p))\subset K_{r_{1}}(\lambda(p))\subset\mathcal{K}_{1},

as
K_{r_{1}}(\lambda(p))\subset\mathcal{K}_{1}
.
∎

The geometric objects and statements of Lemma
5.6
and Lemma
5.7
are visualized in Figure
5.2
. After having studied the initial behavior of the neural DDE
\overline{\Phi}
, we aim to track the behavior until time
T
, which is characterized by an exponential attraction of general solutions of the DDE (
5.3
) to special solutions. By Theorem
4.11
, there exists an initial value problem

\frac{\mathrm{d}z(t)}{\mathrm{d}t}=\widetilde{F}(t,z(t))\coloneqq\overline{F}(t,[\bar{y}(t,z(t))]_{t}),\qquad z(0)=y_{1},

(5.7)

with
\widetilde{F}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
generating all special solutions of the DDE (
5.3
). In analogy to the maps
G
and
H
defined in (
5.4
) and (
5.5
) characterizing the solution map of the DDE (
5.3
) over the time intervals
[0,t^{\ast}]
and
[0,T]
, we define the solution maps of the ODE (
5.7
) over the time intervals
[0,t^{\ast}]
and
[0,T]
as

\displaystyle\overline{G}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m},\qquad\overline{G}(y_{1})=\bar{y}(0,y_{1})(t^{\ast}),

(5.8)

\displaystyle\overline{H}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m},\qquad\overline{H}(y_{1})=\bar{y}(0,y_{1})(T).

(5.9)

Hereby
\bar{y}(0,y_{1})
is the unique solution of the ODE (
5.7
), which is by Theorem
4.11
the unique special solution of the DDE (
5.3
) through the point
(0,y_{1})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
. The following lemma characterizes the distance between the solutions of the DDE (
5.3
) and their corresponding special solutions in terms of the solution maps
G
,
H
,
\overline{G}
, and
\overline{H}
.

Lemma 5.8

(Exponential Attraction)
.

Let the following assumption be satisfied:

•

The underlying vector field
\overline{F}
of the neural DDE
\overline{\Phi}\in\overline{\textup{NDDE}}^{k}_{\tau,\textup{N},K}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
,
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
open,
k\geq 0
, fulfills Assumption
4.2
with global Lipschitz constant
K>0
and it holds
K\tau e<1
.

•

The maps
G
and
H
are the solution maps of the DDE (
5.3
) as defined in (
5.4
), (
5.5
) and the maps
\overline{G}
and
\overline{H}
are the solution maps of the ODE (
5.7
) as defined in (
5.8
) and (
5.9
) with
t^{\ast}=\beta\tau
,
\beta>0
.

•

\mathcal{K}_{1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
\mathcal{K}_{2}=G(\mathcal{K}_{1})
are compact sets and
r_{1}>0
is a constant.

Then there exists a constant
C_{2}\geq\frac{r_{1}}{2}
, such that for every
y_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}
, there exists
\bar{y}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, uniquely determined by the limit (
4.5
) for the initial data
(0,c_{y_{1}})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{C}
, such that

\lVert G(y_{1})-\overline{G}(\bar{y}_{1})\lVert_{\infty}<\delta_{2,\beta}\coloneqq C_{2}e^{-\beta},\qquad\lVert H(y_{1})-\overline{H}(\bar{y}_{1})\lVert_{\infty}<\delta_{3,\tau}\coloneqq C_{2}e^{-\frac{T}{\tau}}.

For
y_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}
it holds

\overline{G}(\overline{y}_{1})\in\mathcal{K}_{2,\delta_{2,\beta}}\coloneqq\left\{y_{2}+\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:y_{2}\in\mathcal{K}_{2},\alpha\in B_{\delta_{2,\beta}}(0)\right\},

with
\mathcal{K}_{2,\delta_{2,\beta}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
open.

[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]

Figure 5.2
:
Visualization of the geometric objects and statements of Lemma
5.6
and Lemma
5.7
: the
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism
\mu
maps the set
K_{r_{0}}(0)
onto the set
\mathcal{K}_{0}
and it holds
p=\mu(0)
. The affine linear map
\lambda
maps in the case that
\text{rank}(W)=n
the set
\mathcal{K}_{0}
onto the set
\mathcal{K}_{1}
, which contains the closed ball
K_{r_{1}}(\lambda(p))
for some
r_{1}>0
. Furthermore, for
\tau
sufficiently small, it holds
G(\lambda(p))\in K_{\kappa/2}(\lambda(p))
for a given constant
0<\kappa\leq r_{1}
.

Proof.

As the underlying vector field
\overline{F}
of the neural DDE
\overline{\Phi}\in\overline{\textup{NDDE}}^{k}_{\tau,\textup{N},K}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
fulfills Assumption
4.2
and
K\tau e<1
, Theorem
4.7
implies that for every initial data
(0,c_{y_{1}})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{C}
specified by
y_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}
, there exists

\bar{y}_{1}\coloneqq\lim_{s\rightarrow\infty}\bar{y}(s,y(0,c_{y_{1}})(s))(0)\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\quad\text{and}\quad C_{y_{1}}\coloneqq\sup_{t\geq 0}e^{\frac{t}{\tau}}\left\lVert y(0,c_{y_{1}})(t)-S(y(0,c_{y_{1}}))(t)\right\rVert_{\infty}<\infty,

where
S(y(0,c_{y_{1}}))=\bar{y}(0,\bar{y}_{1})
is the special solution of the DDE (
5.3
) through the point
(0,\bar{y}_{1})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
. As the upper bound
C_{y_{1}}
depends continuously on the initial function
c_{y_{1}}\in\mathcal{C}
,
C_{y_{1}}
also depends continuously on
y_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}
. As
\mathcal{K}_{1}
is compact, there exists
C_{2}\coloneqq\sup_{y_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}}C_{y_{1}}+\delta
with some constant
\delta>0
, such that
C_{2}\geq\frac{r_{1}}{2}
for the given constant
r_{1}>0
. For every
y_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}
it follows

\displaystyle\lVert G(y_{1})-\overline{G}(\bar{y}_{1})\lVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert y(0,c_{y_{1}})(t^{\ast})-S(y(0,c_{y_{1}}))(t^{\ast})\right\rVert_{\infty}<C_{2}e^{-\frac{t^{\ast}}{\tau}}=C_{2}e^{-\beta}\eqqcolon\delta_{2,\beta},

\displaystyle\lVert H(y_{1})-\overline{H}(\bar{y}_{1})\lVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert y(0,c_{y_{1}})(T)-S(y(0,c_{y_{1}}))(T)\right\rVert_{\infty}<C_{2}e^{-\frac{T}{\tau}}\eqqcolon\delta_{3,\tau}.

As
\overline{G}(\bar{y}_{1})
has distance less than
\delta_{2,\beta}
from
G(y_{1})\in\mathcal{K}_{2}
, it follows that
\overline{G}(\bar{y}_{1})
is contained in the set

\mathcal{K}_{2,\delta_{2,\beta}}\coloneqq\left\{y_{2}+\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:y_{2}\in\mathcal{K}_{2},\alpha\in B_{\delta_{2,\beta}}(0)\right\},

which is an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
as a sum of the compact set
\mathcal{K}_{2}
and the open ball
B_{\delta_{2,\beta}}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
.
∎

The solution maps
\overline{G}
and
\overline{H}
characterize the solutions of the initial value problem (
5.7
) over the time intervals
[0,t^{\ast}]
and
[0,T]
. To additionally characterize the solution of the ODE (
5.7
) over the time interval
[t^{\ast},T]
, we introduce the map
\overline{I}
defined by

\overline{I}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m},\qquad\overline{I}(y_{2})=\bar{y}(t^{\ast},y_{2})(T).

(5.10)

By definition it holds
\overline{H}=\overline{I}\circ\overline{G}
. In Figure
5.1
, the corresponding time intervals of the solution maps
\overline{G}
,
\overline{H}
, and
\overline{I}
are visualized. At time
t=0
, our main analysis is restricted to the compact set
\mathcal{K}_{1}
, which is mapped under the continuous map
G
to the compact set
\mathcal{K}_{2}=G(\mathcal{K}_{1})
. By Lemma
5.8
, all special solutions corresponding to solutions of the DDE (
5.3
) with initial data
(0,c_{y_{1}})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{C}
with
y_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}
have at time
t=t^{\ast}
at most the distance
\delta_{2,\beta}
to the general solutions, so
G(\bar{y}_{1})\in\mathcal{K}_{2,\delta_{2,\beta}}
. The following lemma shows that the map
\overline{I}:\mathcal{K}_{2,\delta_{2,\beta}}\rightarrow\overline{I}(\mathcal{K}_{2,\delta_{2,\beta}})
is a
C^{k}
-diffeomorphism.

Lemma 5.9

(Diffeomorphic Transformation)
.

Let the underlying vector field
\overline{F}
of the neural DDE
\overline{\Phi}\in\overline{\textup{NDDE}}^{k}_{\tau,\textup{N},K}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
,
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
open,
k\geq 1
, fulfill Assumption
4.2
with global Lipschitz constant
K>0
. Let
K\tau e<1
and the map
\overline{I}
be defined by (
5.10
), which is the solution map over the time interval
[t^{\ast},T]
of the ODE (
5.7
) generating all special solutions. Then
\overline{I}\in C^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbb{R}^{m})
and the map
\overline{I}:\mathcal{K}_{2,\delta_{2,\beta}}\rightarrow\overline{I}(\mathcal{K}_{2,\delta_{2,\beta}})
is a
C^{k}
-diffeomorphism. Furthermore, the Jacobian matrix
J_{\overline{I}}(x)
has full rank for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

Proof.

As
\overline{\Phi}\in\overline{\textup{NDDE}}^{k}_{\tau,\textup{N},K}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
,
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
open and
k\geq 1
, it holds
\overline{F}\in C_{b}^{0,k}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{C},\mathbb{R}^{m})
. As every solution of the ODE (
5.7
) is also a solution of the DDE (
5.3
), Lemma
2.5
implies that
\overline{I}\in C^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbb{R}^{m})
. By Theorem
4.11
, the solution curves of the ODE (
5.7
) are unique, such that
\overline{I}:\mathcal{U}\rightarrow\overline{I}(\mathcal{U})
is a
C^{k}
-diffeomorphism for every open subset
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
, especially for
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{K}_{2,\delta_{2,\beta}}
. The chain rule applied to the identity map
\text{id}_{\mathcal{U}}=\overline{I}^{-1}\circ\overline{I}:\mathcal{U}\rightarrow\mathcal{U}
implies that

\left[J_{\overline{I}^{-1}}(\overline{I}(x))\right]^{-1}=J_{\overline{I}}(x)\qquad\text{for all }x\in\mathcal{U},

which shows that
J_{\overline{I}}(x)
is invertible and has full rank for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, as
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
was arbitrary.
∎

5.4
Separation of the Phase Space by Level Sets

After having studied the first affine linear transformation
\lambda
and the DDE dynamics of the neural DDE
\overline{\Phi}\in\overline{\textup{NDDE}}^{k}_{\tau,\textup{N},K}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
via the maps
G
,
H
,
\overline{G}
and
\overline{H}
in the last section, we continue with the second affine linear transformation
\tilde{\lambda}
and the solution map
\bar{I}
in this section. Via the Jordan-Brouwer Separation Theorem, we derive conditions, which are necessary for a neural DDE
\overline{\Phi}\in\overline{\textup{NDDE}}^{k}_{\tau,\textup{N},K}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
in order to approximate in the
i
-th component a given map
\Psi_{i}
with a non-degenerate local extreme point with accuracy
\varepsilon>0
, as defined in Theorem
5.4
. To state the Jordan-Brouwer Separation Theorem, we need to define an orientable hypersurface.

Definition 5.10

(Orientable Hypersurface
[
35
]
)
.

A subset
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
is called a
C^{k}
-hypersurface,
k\geq 1
, if for every
x\in\mathcal{M}
there exists a function
\varphi\in C^{k}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{R})
with
x\in\mathcal{U}
,
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
open, such that
\nabla\varphi(x)\neq 0
for all
x\in\mathcal{U}
and
\varphi^{-1}(z)=\mathcal{M}\cap\mathcal{U}
for some
z\in\mathbb{R}
. A
C^{k}
-hypersurface,
k\geq 1
, is called orientable if there exists a continuous vector field of normal unit vectors, i.e., a map
v\in C^{0}(\mathcal{M},\mathbb{R}^{m})
with
\left\lVert v(x)\right\rVert_{\infty}=1
and
v(x)
is normal to
\mathcal{M}
at
x
for every
x\in\mathcal{M}
.

For closed, orientable hypersurfaces
\mathcal{M}
, we introduce in the following the Jordan-Brouwer Separation Theorem, which states that the complement of
\mathcal{M}
in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
consists of exactly two components. If the hypersurface
\mathcal{M}
is compact, the space
\mathbb{R}^{m}
is separated by
\mathcal{M}
into an interior and an exterior set.

Theorem 5.11

(Jordan-Brouwer Separation Theorem
[
35
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a connected, closed, orientable
C^{k}
-hypersurface,
k\geq 1
. Then its complement
\mathbb{R}^{m}\setminus\mathcal{M}
has two connected open components
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
, each of which has
\mathcal{M}
as its boundary.

The original theorem in
[
35
]
is given under the additional assumption that the hypersurface
\mathcal{M}
is compact and smooth, but the author states in a remark that the same proof applies for closed and continuously differentiable hypersurfaces. In Figure
5.3
, the Jordan-Brouwer Separation Theorem is visualized both for the case that the hypersurface
\mathcal{M}
is compact and the case that
\mathcal{M}
is non-compact.

In Lemma
5.6
we introduced the compact set
\mathcal{K}_{1}\coloneqq\lambda(\mathcal{K}_{0})=\lambda(\mu(K_{r_{0}}(0)))
, where
\lambda:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the first affine linear transformation of the neural DDE for the case that
m=n
and
\mu:\mathcal{U}\rightarrow\mu(\mathcal{U})
is a
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism. The upcoming lemma shows, that its boundary
\mathcal{M}=\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
is a compact
C^{1}
-hypersurface subdividing
\mathbb{R}^{m}\setminus\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
into one open bounded component
\mathcal{M}_{1}=\textup{int}(\mathcal{K}_{1})=\mathcal{K}_{1}\setminus\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
and one open unbounded component
\mathcal{M}_{2}=\mathbb{R}^{m}\setminus\mathcal{K}_{1}
, as visualized in Figure
5.3
3(a)
.

Lemma 5.12

(Phase Space Separation of the Compact Set
\mathcal{K}_{1}
)
.

Let
\overline{\Phi}\in\overline{\textup{NDDE}}^{k}_{\tau,\textup{N},K}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
,
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
open,
k\geq 1
, be a neural DDE and define the compact set
\mathcal{K}_{1}\coloneqq\lambda(\mu(K_{r_{0}}(0)))\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where
\lambda:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\lambda(x)=Wx+b
,
W\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
,
b\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, is the first affine linear transformation of
\overline{\Phi}
with
\textup{rank}(W)=n
and
\mu:\mathcal{U}\rightarrow\mu(\mathcal{U})
,
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
open, is a
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism. Then the boundary
\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
is a compact
C^{1}
-hypersurface and
\mathbb{R}^{m}\setminus\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
consists of one open bounded component
\textup{int}(\mathcal{K}_{1})=\mathcal{K}_{1}\setminus\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
and one open unbounded component
\mathbb{R}^{m}\setminus\mathcal{K}_{1}
.

Proof.

As
\textup{rank}(W)=n
, both maps
\mu:\mathcal{U}\rightarrow\mu(\mathcal{U})
and
\lambda:\mu(\mathcal{U})\rightarrow\lambda(\mu(\mathcal{U}))
are
C^{1}
-diffeomorphisms, such that it follows

\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}=\partial\lambda(\mu(K_{r_{0}}(0)))=\lambda(\mu(\partial K_{r_{0}}(0))).

By Definition
5.10
,
\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
is a
C^{1}
-hypersurface: the map

\varphi:\lambda(\mu(\mathcal{U}))\setminus\lambda(\mu(0))\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\quad\varphi(x)=\left\lVert\mu^{-1}(\lambda^{-1}(x))\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

is defined on the open set
\mathcal{V}\coloneqq\lambda(\mu(\mathcal{U}))\setminus\lambda(\mu(0))
,
\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}\subset\mathcal{V}
,
\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}=\varphi^{-1}(r_{0})
and by the chain rule
\varphi
is continuously differentiable. Furthermore,

\nabla\varphi(x)=\left[2\mu^{-1}(\lambda^{-1}(x))\cdot J_{\mu^{-1}}(\lambda^{-1}(x))\cdot J_{\lambda^{-1}}(x)\right]^{\top}\neq 0\qquad\text{for all }x\in\mathcal{V},

as
2\mu^{-1}(\lambda^{-1}(x))\neq 0
for
x\in\mathcal{V}
and the Jacobian matrices
J_{\mu^{-1}}
and
J_{\lambda^{-1}}
have for every point in their respective domain of definition full rank, as
\mu
and
\lambda
are diffeomorphisms. The hypersurface
\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
is orientable, as
v:\mathcal{K}_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
v(x)\coloneqq\frac{\nabla\varphi(x)}{\left\lVert\nabla\varphi(x)\right\rVert_{\infty}}
is a continuous vector field of normal unit vectors. The hypersurface
\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
is closed, as it is the pre-image of the closed set
\{r_{0}\}\subset\mathbb{R}
under the continuous map
\varphi
. The Jordan-Brouwer-Separation Theorem
5.11
implies that
\mathbb{R}^{m}\setminus\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
consists of two connected open components: as
\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
is compact, the interior component
\textup{int}(\mathcal{K}_{1})=\mathcal{K}_{1}\setminus\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
is bounded and the exterior component
\mathbb{R}^{m}\setminus\mathcal{K}_{1}
is unbounded.
∎

(a)

If the hypersurface
\mathcal{M}
is compact,
\mathbb{R}^{m}\setminus\mathcal{M}
consists of one bounded component
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and one unbounded component
\mathcal{M}_{2}
.

.

(b)

If the hypersurface
\mathcal{M}
is non-compact, the complement
\mathbb{R}^{m}\setminus\mathcal{M}
consists of two unbounded components
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
.

Figure 5.3
:
Visualization of the Jordan-Brouwer Separation Theorem
5.11
.

As our main Theorem
5.4
treats the
i
-th component of a given map
\Psi_{i}
, we restrict our upcoming analysis to the
i
-th component of the neural DDE
\overline{\Phi}
, which is calculated as

\overline{\Phi}_{i}=\tilde{\lambda}_{i}\circ H\circ\lambda.

The
i
-th component of the affine linear map
\tilde{\lambda}
is the affine linear map
\tilde{\lambda}_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
\lambda_{i}(y)=[\widetilde{W}y]_{i}+\tilde{b}_{i}
. If we assume, that the
i
-th row of the weight matrix
\widetilde{W}
is non-zero, then the pre-image of
z\in\mathbb{R}
under the map
\tilde{\lambda}_{i}

\tilde{\lambda}_{i}^{-1}\left(z\right)\coloneqq\left\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:\tilde{\lambda}_{i}(y)=z\right\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}

(5.11)

is by definition a smooth hyperplane, a special case of a smooth hypersurface. As the map
\overline{I}
defined in (
5.10
) is by Lemma
5.9
a
C^{k}
-diffeomorphism, the pre-image of
\tilde{\lambda}^{-1}\left(z\right)
under the map
\overline{I}
is an unbounded
C^{k}
-hypersurface
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{H}_{z}
, which separates the space
\mathbb{R}^{m}
in two unbounded components
\mathcal{M}_{1}=\mathcal{H}_{z}^{<}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}=\mathcal{H}_{z}^{>}
, as visualized in Figure
5.3
3(b)
.

Lemma 5.13

(Separating Hypersurfaces)
.

Let
\overline{\Phi}\in\overline{\textup{NDDE}}^{k}_{\tau,\textup{N},K}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
,
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
open,
k\geq 1
, be a neural DDE with underlying vector field
\overline{F}
satisfying Assumption
4.2
. Let
\tilde{\lambda}_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
\lambda_{i}(y)=[\widetilde{W}y]_{i}+\tilde{b}_{i}
be an affine linear map with
\widetilde{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times m}
,
\tilde{b}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
and
[\widetilde{W}^{\top}]_{i}\neq 0
and let
\overline{I}
be the map defined in (
5.10
). Then

\mathcal{H}_{z}\coloneqq\overline{I}^{-1}(\tilde{\lambda}_{i}^{-1}(z))

is a closed, unbounded
C^{k}
-hypersurface and
\mathbb{R}^{m}\setminus\mathcal{H}_{z}
consists of two connected unbounded open components
\mathcal{H}_{z}^{<}
and
\mathcal{H}_{z}^{>}
with
\mathcal{H}_{z}
as their boundary. If
y\in\mathcal{H}_{z}^{<}
, then
\tilde{\lambda}_{i}(\overline{I}(y))<z
and if
y\in\mathcal{H}_{z}^{>}
, then
\tilde{\lambda}_{i}(\overline{I}(y))>z
. The closure of the open sets
\mathcal{H}_{z}^{<}
and
\mathcal{H}_{z}^{>}
is denoted by
\mathcal{H}_{z}^{\leq}\coloneqq\mathcal{H}_{z}^{<}\cup\mathcal{H}_{z}
and
\mathcal{H}_{z}^{\geq}\coloneqq\mathcal{H}_{z}^{>}\cup\mathcal{H}_{z}
, respectively.

Proof.

By Definition
5.10
,
\mathcal{H}_{z}
is a
C^{k}
-hypersurface: the map
\varphi\coloneqq\tilde{\lambda}_{i}\circ\overline{I}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is
k
times continuously differentiable, as
\tilde{\lambda}_{i}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbb{R})
and
\overline{I}\in C^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbb{R}^{m})
by Lemma
5.9
. For
z\in\mathbb{R}
it holds
\mathcal{H}_{z}=\varphi^{-1}(z)
and the chain rule implies

\nabla\varphi(x)=J_{\overline{I}}(x)^{\top}\cdot[\widetilde{W}^{\top}]_{i}\neq 0\qquad\text{for all }x\in\mathbb{R}^{m},

as by assumption
[\widetilde{W}^{\top}]_{i}\neq 0
and by Lemma
5.9
the Jacobian
J_{\overline{I}}(x)
has full rank for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
. The hypersurface
\mathcal{H}_{z}
is orientable, as
v:\mathcal{H}_{z}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
v(x)\coloneqq\frac{\nabla\varphi(x)}{\left\lVert\nabla\varphi(x)\right\rVert_{\infty}}
is a continuous vector field of normal unit vectors. The hypersurface
\mathcal{H}_{z}
is closed, as it is the pre-image of the closed set
\{z\}\subset\mathbb{R}
under the continuous map
\varphi
. As
\mathcal{H}_{z}
is unbounded, Theorem
5.11
implies that
\mathbb{R}^{m}\setminus\mathcal{H}_{z}
consists of two connected unbounded open components
\mathcal{H}_{z}^{<}
and
\mathcal{H}_{z}^{>}
with
\mathcal{H}_{z}
as their boundary.

Let
y\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\setminus\mathcal{H}_{z}
with
\varphi(y)>z
, define the open component of
\mathbb{R}^{m}\setminus\mathcal{H}_{z}
in which
y
is included to be
\mathcal{H}_{z}^{>}
. Let
w\in\mathcal{H}_{z}^{>}
with
w\neq y
, then it also holds
\varphi(w)>z
: assume by contradiction that
\varphi(w)<z
. Then it follows

[\widetilde{W}\overline{I}(w)]_{i}+\tilde{b}_{i}=\varphi(w)<z<\varphi(y)=[\widetilde{W}\overline{I}(y)]_{i}+\tilde{b}_{i},

such that the points
\overline{I}(w)
and
\overline{I}(y)
are separated by the hyperplane
\mathcal{H}_{z}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:[\widetilde{W}x]_{i}+\tilde{b}_{i}=z\}
. As the map
\overline{I}
is continuous, the fact that
w
and
y
are in the same connected component
\mathcal{H}_{z}
implies that
\overline{I}(w)
and
\overline{I}(y)
are in the same connected component, which is a contradiction to the assumption
\varphi(w)>z
. Consequently,
\varphi(y)>z
if
y\in\mathcal{H}_{z}^{>}
and
\varphi(y)<z
if
y\in\mathcal{H}_{z}^{<}
.
∎

In the following, we prove a result about the relationship between the compact hypersurface
\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
introduced in Lemma
5.12
and the unbounded hypersurface
\mathcal{H}_{z}
introduced in Lemma
5.13
.

Lemma 5.14

(Existence of an Intersection Point of
\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\leq}_{z}
)
.

Let
\mathcal{K}_{1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a compact set and its boundary
\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
be a compact
C^{1}
-hypersurface. Denote the bounded open component of
\mathbb{R}^{m}\setminus\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
by
\textup{int}(\mathcal{K}_{1})=\mathcal{K}_{1}\setminus\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
and the open unbounded component by
\mathbb{R}^{m}\setminus\mathcal{K}_{1}
. Let the assumptions of Lemma
5.13
be fulfilled and let
\mathcal{H}_{z}
be the unbounded
C^{k}
-hypersurface defined therein for a given
z\in\mathbb{R}
. Assume there is a point
y_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}
with
y_{1}\in\mathcal{H}^{<}_{z}
, then it follows that there exists
y_{1}^{\ast}\in\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
with
y_{1}^{\ast}\in\mathcal{H}^{\leq}_{z}
.

Proof.

\mathcal{H}_{z}
is by Lemma
5.13
a closed, unbounded
C^{k}
-hypersurface with
k\geq 1
. As the hypersurface
\mathcal{H}_{z}
is unbounded and
\mathcal{K}_{1}
is compact and hence bounded, it is impossible that
\mathcal{H}_{z}\subset\mathcal{K}_{1}
. Consequently it either holds
\mathcal{H}_{z}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}\setminus\mathcal{K}_{1}
or
\mathcal{H}_{z}\cap\mathcal{K}_{1}\neq\varnothing
, see Figure
5.4
.

If
\mathcal{H}_{z}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}\setminus\mathcal{K}_{1}
, then
\mathcal{K}_{1}
is completely on one of the two sides of the hypersurface, hence
\mathcal{K}_{1}\subset\mathcal{H}_{z}^{<}
or
\mathcal{K}_{1}\subset\mathcal{H}_{z}^{>}
. As by assumption there exists a point
y_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}
with
y_{1}\in\mathcal{H}^{<}_{z}
, it follows that
\mathcal{K}_{1}\subset\mathcal{H}_{z}^{<}\subset\mathcal{H}_{z}^{\leq}
. The statement of the lemma follows for every point
y^{\ast}_{1}\in\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
as
\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}\subset\mathcal{K}_{1}
, see Figure
5.4
4(a)

If
\mathcal{H}_{z}\cap\mathcal{K}_{1}\neq\varnothing
, then also
\mathcal{H}_{z}\cap\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}\neq\varnothing
as
\mathcal{K}_{1}
is closed, hence there exists
y^{\ast}_{1}\in\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
with
y_{1}^{\ast}\in\mathcal{H}_{z}\subset\mathcal{H}_{z}^{\leq}
, as visualized in Figure
5.4
4(b)
.
∎

(a)

It holds
y_{1}\in\mathcal{H}_{z}^{<}
and
\mathcal{H}_{z}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}\setminus\mathcal{K}_{1}
, hence every point
y^{\ast}_{1}\in\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
fulfills
y_{1}^{\ast}\in\mathcal{H}^{\leq}_{z}
.

.

(b)

It holds
\mathcal{H}_{z}\cap\mathcal{K}_{1}\neq\varnothing
, hence there exists
y^{\ast}_{1}\in\mathcal{H}_{z}\cap\mathcal{K}_{1}\neq\varnothing
and it holds
y_{1}^{\ast}\in\mathcal{H}^{\leq}_{z}
.

Figure 5.4
:
Visualization of the proof of Lemma
5.14
.

In addition to pre-images of points under the map
\tilde{\lambda}_{i}
defined in (
5.11
), we also define the pre-image of an open interval

\tilde{\lambda}_{i}^{-1}\left((c,d)\right)\coloneqq\left\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:\tilde{\lambda}_{i}(y)\in(c,d)\right\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m},

(5.12)

which is an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
.
In our main Theorem
5.4
, we study the approximation of a given component map
\Psi_{i}
by the
i
-th component of the neural DDE, denoted by
\overline{\Phi}_{i}
. By Lemma
5.6
, the map
\Psi_{i}:\mathcal{K}_{0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
attains its global minimum at
p
with value
\Psi_{i}(p)
and its global maximum at the boundary
\partial\mathcal{K}_{0}
with value
\Psi_{i}(p)+r_{0}^{2}
. To study, if
\Psi_{i}
can be approximated by
\overline{\Phi}_{i}
with accuracy
\varepsilon
, we define the following pre-images under the map
\tilde{\lambda}_{i}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{3}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\tilde{\lambda}^{-1}_{i}\left((\Psi_{i}(p)-\varepsilon,\Psi_{i}(p)+\varepsilon)\right)\subset\mathbb{R}^{m},

(5.13)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{3}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\tilde{\lambda}^{-1}_{i}\left((\Psi_{i}(p)+r_{0}^{2}-\varepsilon,\Psi_{i}(p)+r_{0}^{2}+\varepsilon)\right)\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}.

(5.14)

In addition, we define for the
\tau
-dependent constant
\delta_{3,\tau}
of Lemma
5.8
the extensions of the pre-images
\mathcal{Y}_{3}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{3}
, given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\tilde{\lambda}_{i}^{-1}\left((\Psi_{i}(p)-\varepsilon-\delta_{3,\tau},\Psi_{i}(p)+\varepsilon+\delta_{3,\tau})\right)

(5.15)

\displaystyle=\left\{z+\alpha:z\in\mathcal{Y}_{3},\alpha\in B_{\delta_{3,\tau}}(0)\right\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m},

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\tilde{\lambda}^{-1}_{i}\left((\Psi_{i}(p)+r_{0}^{2}-\varepsilon-\delta_{3,\tau},\Psi_{i}(p)+r_{0}^{2}+\varepsilon+\delta_{3,\tau})\right)

(5.16)

\displaystyle=\left\{z+\alpha:z\in\mathcal{Z}_{3},\alpha\in B_{\delta_{3,\tau}}(0)\right\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}.

In the following Lemma we show that under the assumption
0<2\varepsilon<r_{0}^{2}
of our main Theorem
5.4
, there exists an upper bound
\tau_{3}
, such that for all
\tau\in[0,\tau_{3}]
, the sets
\mathcal{Y}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau}}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau}}
are disjoint. The sets
\mathcal{Y}_{3}
,
\mathcal{Z}_{3}
,
\mathcal{Y}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau_{3}}}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau_{3}}}
are visualized in Figure
5.5
.

Lemma 5.15

(Positive Distance between
\mathcal{Y}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau}}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau}}
)
.

Let
0<2\varepsilon<r_{0}^{2}
and
\tilde{\lambda}_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
\tilde{\lambda}_{i}(y)=[\widetilde{W}y]_{i}+\tilde{b}_{i}
be an affine linear map with
\widetilde{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times m}
,
\tilde{b}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
and
[\widetilde{W}^{\top}]_{i}\neq 0
. Let the assumptions of Lemma
5.13
be fulfilled and let
\mathcal{H}_{z}
be the unbounded
C^{k}
-hypersurface defined therein. Then there exists
\tau_{3}>0
, such that for all
\tau\in[0,\tau_{3}]
it holds

\mathcal{Y}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau}}\subset\mathcal{Y}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau_{3}}},\qquad\mathcal{Z}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau}}\subset\mathcal{Z}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau_{3}}},\qquad\mathcal{Y}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau}}=\varnothing

and

\big\lVert\tilde{\lambda}_{i}(y_{3})-\tilde{\lambda}_{i}(z_{3})\big\lVert_{\infty}\geq r_{0}^{2}-2\varepsilon-2\delta_{3,\tau_{3}}\eqqcolon\delta^{\ast}>0

for all
y_{3}\in\mathcal{Y}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau}}
and
z_{3}\in\mathcal{Z}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau}}
. Furthermore, it holds

\big\lVert\tilde{\lambda}_{i}(\overline{I}(y_{2}))-\tilde{\lambda}_{i}(\overline{I}(z_{2}))\big\lVert_{\infty}\geq\delta^{\ast}>0

for all
y_{2}\in\mathcal{H}^{\leq}_{\Psi_{i}(p)+\varepsilon+\delta_{3,\tau_{3}}}
and
z_{2}\in\mathcal{H}^{\geq}_{\Psi_{i}(p)+r_{0}^{2}-\varepsilon-\delta_{3,\tau_{3}}}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.8
it holds
\delta_{3,\tau}=C_{2}e^{-\frac{T}{\tau}}
for some constant
C_{2}\geq\frac{r_{1}}{2}>0
. Let
0<\delta^{\ast}<r_{0}^{2}-2\varepsilon
, which exists as by assumption
0<2\varepsilon<r_{0}^{2}
, and define
\tau_{3}\coloneqq\frac{T}{\ln\left(\frac{2C_{2}}{r_{0}^{2}-2\varepsilon-\delta^{\ast}}\right)}>0
. Then it holds for all
\tau\in[0,\tau_{3}]
that

r_{0}^{2}-2\varepsilon-2\delta_{3,\tau}\geq r_{0}^{2}-2\varepsilon-2\delta_{3,\tau_{3}}=r_{0}^{2}-2\varepsilon-2C_{2}\cdot\frac{r_{0}^{2}-2\varepsilon-\delta^{\ast}}{2C_{2}}=\delta^{\ast}>0.

As it holds by definitions (
5.15
) and (
5.16
) that

\mathcal{Y}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau}}\subset\mathcal{Y}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau_{3}}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{Z}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau}}\subset\mathcal{Z}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau_{3}}},

it follows
\mathcal{Y}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau}}=\varnothing
for every
\tau\in[0,\tau_{3}]
and

\big\lVert\tilde{\lambda}_{i}(y_{3})-\tilde{\lambda}_{i}(z_{3})\big\lVert_{\infty}=r_{0}^{2}-2\varepsilon-2\delta_{3,\tau}\geq r_{0}^{2}-2\varepsilon-2\delta_{3,\tau_{3}}=\delta^{\ast}>0

for all
y_{3}\in\mathcal{Y}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau}}
and
z_{3}\in\mathcal{Z}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau}}
. By the definition of the halfspaces
\mathcal{H}_{z}^{\leq}
and
\mathcal{H}_{z}^{\geq}
of Lemma
5.13
it follows for
y_{2}\in\mathcal{H}^{\leq}_{\Psi_{i}(p)+\varepsilon+\delta_{3,\tau_{3}}}
and
z_{2}\in\mathcal{H}^{\geq}_{\Psi_{i}(p)+r_{0}^{2}-\varepsilon-\delta_{3,\tau_{3}}}
that

\big\lVert\tilde{\lambda}_{i}(\overline{I}(y_{2}))-\tilde{\lambda}_{i}(\overline{I}(z_{2}))\big\lVert_{\infty}\geq\delta^{\ast}>0.\qed

Figure 5.5
:
Visualization of the sets
\mathcal{Y}_{3}
,
\mathcal{Z}_{3}
,
\mathcal{Y}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau_{3}}}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau_{3}}}
. By Lemma
5.15
, the sets
\mathcal{Y}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau}}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau}}
have for all
\tau\in[0,\tau_{3}]
a positive distance.

5.5
Proof of the Main Result

In this section, we combine the results obtained in the previous Sections
5.3
and
5.4
to prove Theorem
5.4
, which is stated for better readability here again.

Theorem
5.4

(No Approximation of Non-Degenerate Local Extreme Points)
.

Let
\Psi:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{q}
,
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
open, have a component map
\Psi_{i}\in C^{3}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R})
, which has a non-degenerate local extreme point with radius of definition
r_{0}>0
and let
\varepsilon>0
with
2\varepsilon<r_{0}^{2}
. Furthermore, fix constants
K,w,\tilde{w}\in(0,\infty)
,
A\geq 0
and
k\geq 1
.

Then there exists a continuous function
\tau_{0}\in C^{0}((0,\infty),[0,T])
with
K\tau_{0}(K)e<1
for
K\in(0,\infty)
with the following property: if
\tau\in[0,\tau_{0}(K))
, every neural DDE
\Phi\in\textup{NDDE}_{\tau,\textup{N},K}^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
with

•

vector field
F:\Omega_{t}\times\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
with global Lipschitz constant
K
in its second variable on
\Omega_{t}\times\mathcal{C}
and
\left\lVert F(t,0)\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq A
for all
t\in[0,T]
,

•

weight matrices
W,\widetilde{W}
with
\big\lVert W\big\lVert_{\infty}\leq w
and
\big\lVert\widetilde{W}\big\lVert_{\infty}\leq\tilde{w}
,

cannot approximate the map
\Psi
with accuracy
\varepsilon
, i.e., for every neural DDE
\Phi\in\textup{NDDE}_{\tau,\textup{N},K}^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
, there exists a point
x\in\mathcal{X}
, such that

\left\lVert\Phi(x)-\Psi(x)\right\rVert_{\infty}\geq\varepsilon.

Proof.

We subdivide the proof into seven steps.

1.

Restriction to non-degenerate local minima:
Let
\Psi:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{q}
,
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
open, have a component map
\Psi_{i}\in C^{3}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R})
, which has a non-degenerate local extreme point
p\in\mathcal{X}
with radius of definition
r_{0}>0
. By Definitions
5.1
and
5.3
, there exists a neighborhood
\mathcal{U}
of
0\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
K_{r_{0}}(0)\subset\mathcal{U}
for some
r_{0}>0
and a
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism
\mu:\mathcal{U}\rightarrow\mu(\mathcal{U})\subset\mathcal{X}
with
\mu(0)=p
, such that for
(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n})\in\mathcal{U}

\Psi_{i}(\mu(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}))=\Psi_{i}(p)\pm\sum_{j=1}^{n}u_{j}^{2},

(5.17)

as the non-degenerate local extreme point
p\in\mathcal{X}
is a critical point with index
r=0
in case of a local minimum and index
r=n
in case of a local maximum. The case
r=0
corresponds to a plus sign in (
5.17
), the case
r=n
to a minus sign in.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that the extreme point
p\in\mathcal{X}
is a local minimum; otherwise, the proof is carried out for the approximation of the map
-\Psi
by a neural DDE. A map
\Psi
can be approximated by a neural DDE
\Phi\in\textup{NDDE}_{\tau,\textup{N},K}^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
if and only if
-\Psi
can be approximated by some neural DDE
\Phi_{-}\in\textup{NDDE}_{\tau,\textup{N},K}^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
: given a neural DDE
\Phi\in\textup{NDDE}_{\tau,\textup{N},K}^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
approximating a given map
\Psi
with accuracy
\varepsilon
, i.e.,

\left\lVert\Phi(x)-\Psi(x)\right\rVert_{\infty}<\varepsilon\qquad\text{for all }x\in\mathcal{X},

then the neural DDE
\Phi_{-}\in\textup{NDDE}_{\tau,\textup{N},K}^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
, which is constructed by replacing the last affine linear layer
\tilde{\lambda}
by
-\tilde{\lambda}
, approximates
-\Psi
with accuracy
\varepsilon
and vice versa.

2.

Restriction to the case
m=n
and parameters in
\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
:
In this theorem, we study non-augmented neural DDEs
\Phi\in\textup{NDDE}_{\tau,\textup{N},K}^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
, i.e., it holds
n\geq m
. It is sufficient to restrict the upcoming analysis to the case
m=n
: if for a given map
\Psi
no approximation with accuracy
\varepsilon
is possible for an underlying DDE in dimension
m=n
, then also no approximation with accuracy
\varepsilon
is possible for an underlying DDE in dimension
m<n
, as every vector field in dimension
m<n
can be extended to a vector field in dimension
n
by adding zero components, which do not change the input-output map
\Phi
.

Due to the assumption
m=n
, the first affine linear map of the neural DDE architecture is a map
\lambda:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\lambda(x)=Wx+b
. If the weight matrix
W\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
has not full rank
n
, then there exist by Lemma
5.6
(c) a point
s\in\partial\mathcal{K}_{0}
with
\lambda(s)=\lambda(p)
, where
\mathcal{K}_{0}\coloneqq\mu(K_{r_{0}}(0))\subset\mathcal{X}
. For every neural DDE
\Phi\in\textup{NDDE}_{\tau,\textup{N},K}^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
it follows
\Phi(p)=\Phi(s)
, as after the affine linear transformation
\lambda
the same initial data is used for the underlying DDE as
\lambda(s)=\lambda(p)
. By Lemma
5.6
(a) it holds
\Psi_{i}(s)=\Psi_{i}(p)+r_{0}^{2}
, which implies

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\left\lVert\Phi(x)-\Psi(x)\right\rVert_{\infty}\geq\sup_{x\in\mathcal{K}_{0}}\left\lvert\Phi_{i}(x)-\Psi_{i}(x)\right\rvert\geq\max_{x\in\{p,s\}}\left\lvert\Phi_{i}(x)-\Psi_{i}(x)\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\max\left\{\left\lvert\Phi_{i}(p)-\Psi_{i}(p)\right\rvert,\left\lvert\Phi_{i}(p)-\Psi_{i}(p)-r_{0}^{2}\right\rvert\right\}\geq\frac{r_{0}^{2}}{2}>\varepsilon,

as by assumption
2\varepsilon<r_{0}^{2}
. Hence, Theorem
5.4
holds for weight matrices
W
with
\text{rank}(W)<n
, such that we restrict the upcoming analysis to the case that
\text{rank}(W)=n
.

The second affine linear map of the neural DDE architecture is a map
\tilde{\lambda}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{q}
,
\tilde{\lambda}(x)=\widetilde{W}x+\tilde{b}
. In the upcoming analysis, we assume that the
i
-th row of the weight matrix
\widetilde{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times m}
, denoted by
[\widetilde{W}^{\top}]_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
is not the zero vector. If
[\widetilde{W}^{\top}]_{i}=0
, then the output of the
i
-th component
\Phi_{i}
of every neural DDE
\Phi\in\textup{NDDE}_{\tau,\textup{N},K}^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
would be constant
\Phi_{i}(x)=\tilde{b}_{i}
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
, as
\tilde{\lambda}_{i}
then becomes the constant function
x\mapsto\tilde{b}_{i}
. A constant component
\Phi_{i}
of the neural DDE cannot approximate the given map
\Psi_{i}
with accuracy
\varepsilon
, as

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\left\lVert\Phi(x)-\Psi(x)\right\rVert_{\infty}\geq\sup_{x\in\mathcal{K}_{0}}\left\lvert\Phi_{i}(x)-\Psi_{i}(x)\right\rvert\geq\sup_{x\in\{p,\partial\mathcal{K}_{0}\}}\left\lvert\tilde{b}_{i}-\Psi_{i}(x)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\geq\;

\displaystyle\max\left\{\left\lvert\tilde{b}_{i}-\Psi_{i}(p)\right\rvert,\left\lvert\tilde{b}_{i}-\Psi_{i}(p)-r_{0}^{2}\right\rvert\right\}\geq\frac{r_{0}^{2}}{2}>\varepsilon,

where we used again Lemma
5.6
(a) that
\Psi_{i}(x)=\Psi_{i}(p)+r_{0}^{2}
for all
x\in\partial\mathcal{K}_{0}
and
2\varepsilon<r_{0}^{2}
by assumption. Hence, Theorem
5.4
holds for weight matrices
\widetilde{W}
with
[\widetilde{W}^{\top}]_{i}=0
, such that we restrict the upcoming analysis to the case that
[\widetilde{W}^{\top}]_{i}\neq 0
. Restricted to the
i
-th component of the neural DDE
\Phi
, the assumptions
\text{rank}(W)=n
and
[\widetilde{W}^{\top}]_{i}\neq 0
imply that the weights and biases are restricted to the parameter space
\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
, cf. Definition
2.8
.

3.

Change to a globally defined vector field:
In this theorem, we study neural DDEs
\Phi\in\textup{NDDE}_{\tau,\textup{N},K}^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
,
k\geq 1
, with vector field
F:\Omega_{t}\times\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
with Lipschitz constant
K
in the second variable on
\Omega_{t}\times\mathcal{C}
and
\left\lVert F(t,0)\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq A
for all
t\in[0,T]
. By Theorem
4.14
, there exists a neural DDE
\overline{\Phi}\in\overline{\textup{NDDE}}_{\tau,\textup{N},K}^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
, such that
\Phi(x)=\overline{\Phi}(x)
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
, which is based on a vector field
\overline{F}\in C_{b}^{0,k}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{C},\mathbb{R}^{m})
fulfilling Assumption
4.2
with with constants
K>0
,
A\geq 0
, and

\overline{F}\big|_{[0,T]\times\mathcal{C}}=F\big|_{[0,T]\times\mathcal{C}}.

From now on, we study the neural DDE
\overline{\Phi}
with globally defined vector field
\overline{F}\in C_{b}^{0,k}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{C},\mathbb{R}^{m})
. By Theorem
4.14
, the final result directly holds for all neural DDEs
\Phi\in\textup{NDDE}_{\tau,\textup{N},K}^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
,
k\geq 1
, which are considered in this theorem. The dynamics of the neural DDE
\overline{\Phi}
is subdivided into three maps

\overline{\Phi}=\tilde{\lambda}\circ H\circ\lambda,

with affine linear transformations
\lambda
,
\tilde{\lambda}
and the solution map
H
of the underlying DDE (
5.3
) over the time interval
[0,T]
as defined in (
5.4
). The solution map of the DDE (
5.3
) over the time interval
[0,t^{\ast}]
with
t^{\ast}\in[0,T)
and
t^{\ast}=\beta\tau
for some
\beta>0
specified in the following is denoted by
G:\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
as defined in (
5.5
). Given
\beta>0
, we need to choose
\tau<\frac{T}{\beta}
to guarantee a well defined solution map
G
on the time interval
[0,t^{\ast}]
.

4.

Analysis of the initial behavior via the map
G
:
As we assume by point 2.) that
m=n
and
\text{rank}(W)=n
, Lemma
5.6
(b) implies that there exists
r_{1}>0
, only depending on the constant
w
, such that
K_{r_{1}}(\lambda(p))\subset\mathcal{K}_{1}
, where
\mathcal{K}_{1}\coloneqq\lambda(\mathcal{K}_{0})\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
is compact and
\mathcal{K}_{0}\coloneqq\mu(K_{r_{0}}(0))
. Furthermore, the component map
\Psi_{i}\circ\lambda^{-1}:\mathcal{K}_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
attains its global minimum at
\lambda(p)
with value
\Psi_{i}(p)
and its global maximum at every point of the boundary
\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
with value
\Psi_{i}(p)+r_{0}^{2}
. After the first affine linear layer
\lambda
, the initial behavior of the DDE solution can be characterized via the solution map
G
over the time interval
[0,t^{\ast}]
, as defined in (
5.5
). In our setting, all assumptions of Lemma
5.7
are fulfilled. Let
\delta_{1,t^{\ast}}\coloneqq C_{1}t^{\ast}
with some constant
C_{1}>0
, which only depends on the given parameters
K,A,T
and
M\coloneqq\sup_{y_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}}\left\lVert y_{1}\right\rVert_{\infty}
, hence
C_{1}
is independent of the choice of
\tau
and
\beta
. From Lemma
5.7
it follows that for every
\beta>0
and every
0<\kappa\leq r_{1}
, there exists an upper bound
\tau_{1,\beta,\kappa}=\frac{\kappa}{2C_{1}\beta}>0
, such that for all
\tau\in[0,\tau_{1,\beta,\kappa}]
and every
y_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}
it holds
G(y_{1})\in\mathcal{K}_{2}\coloneqq G(\mathcal{K}_{1})
and

\left\lVert G(y_{1})-y_{1}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\delta_{1,t^{\ast}}\leq\frac{\kappa}{2}.

(5.18)

Especially for
\lambda(p)\in\mathcal{K}_{1}
we have

G(\lambda(p))\in K_{\delta_{1,t^{\ast}}}(\lambda(p))\subset K_{r_{1}}(\lambda(p))\subset\mathcal{K}_{1}.

(5.19)

5.

Exponential attraction towards special solutions:
After having analyzed the dynamics of the DDE (
5.3
) on the time interval
[0,t^{\ast}]
via the map
G
, we study now the solution map of the DDE (
5.3
) on the time interval
[t^{\ast},T]
, which is characterized by an exponential attraction of general solutions towards special solutions. Special solutions are characterized by an exponential growth bound and a single initial condition and can be obtained as solutions of the initial value problem (
5.7
). Assume
\tau<\frac{1}{Ke}
, then
K\tau e<1
and all assumptions of Lemma
5.8
are fulfilled. In the following we denote by
\overline{G}
and
\overline{H}
the solution maps of the ODE (
5.7
) over the time intervals
[0,t^{\ast}]
and
[0,T]
as defined in (
5.8
) and (
5.9
). By Lemma
5.8
, there exists for the considered radius
r_{1}>0
of point 4.) a constant
C_{2}\geq\frac{r_{1}}{2}>0
, such that for every
y_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}
there exists
\bar{y}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, uniquely determined by the limit (
4.5
) for the initial data
(0,c_{y_{1}})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{C}
with

\displaystyle\lVert G(y_{1})-\overline{G}(\bar{y}_{1})\lVert_{\infty}<\delta_{2,\beta}

\displaystyle\coloneqq C_{2}e^{-\beta},

(5.20)

\displaystyle\lVert H(y_{1})-\overline{H}(\bar{y}_{1})\lVert_{\infty}<\delta_{3,\tau}

\displaystyle\coloneqq C_{2}e^{-\frac{T}{\tau}}.

(5.21)

These inequalities estimate the difference between the considered solution of the DDE (
5.3
) and the corresponding special solution
S(y(0,c_{y_{1}}))=\bar{y}(0,\bar{y}_{1})
towards which it converges, via the maps
G
and
\overline{G}
at time
t=t^{\ast}
, and via the maps
H
and
\overline{H}
at time
t=T
. For every
y_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}
it holds

\overline{G}(\overline{y}_{1})\in\mathcal{K}_{2,\delta_{2,\beta}}\coloneqq\left\{y_{2}+\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:y_{2}\in\mathcal{K}_{2},\alpha\in B_{\delta_{2,\beta}}(0)\right\},

with
\mathcal{K}_{2,\delta_{2,\beta}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
open. In the following, we aim to combine the estimate (
5.20
) with the statements (
5.18
) and (
5.19
). To that purpose, let

0<\kappa\leq r_{1},\quad\beta=\beta_{\kappa}\coloneqq\ln\left(\frac{2C_{2}}{\kappa}\right)\geq 0\quad\text{and}\quad 0\leq\tau<\min\left\{\tau_{1,\beta,\kappa},\frac{1}{Ke},\frac{T}{\beta}\right\}.

(5.22)

Hereby
\beta_{\kappa}
depends on
\kappa
and the previously defined constant
C_{2}
with
2C_{2}\geq r_{1}\geq\kappa>0
and
\tau_{1,\beta,\kappa}=\frac{\kappa}{2C_{1}\beta}>0
is the upper bound of Lemma
5.7
necessary for the estimates (
5.18
), (
5.19
). The upper bound
\frac{1}{Ke}
is necessary for the exponential estimates (
5.20
) and (
5.21
) and the upper bound
\frac{T}{\beta}
guarantees that
t^{\ast}=\beta\tau<T
.
Then it follows from (
5.18
) and (
5.20
) that

\left\lVert\overline{G}(\bar{y}_{1})-y_{1}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\left\lVert\overline{G}(\bar{y}_{1})-G(y_{1})\right\rVert_{\infty}+\left\lVert G(y_{1})-y_{1}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\delta_{1,t^{\ast}}+\delta_{2,\beta}<\kappa,

(5.23)

as

\delta_{1,t^{\ast}}+\delta_{2,\beta}=C_{1}\beta\tau+C_{2}e^{-\beta}<C_{1}\beta\tau_{1,\beta,\kappa}+C_{2}e^{-\beta_{\kappa}}=\frac{\kappa}{2}+\frac{\kappa}{2}=\kappa.

Especially for
\lambda(p)\in\mathcal{K}_{1}
of (
5.19
) it follows
that there exists a point
\overline{\lambda(p)}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
specifying the corresponding special solution
S(y(0,c_{\lambda(p)}))=\bar{y}\big(0,\overline{\lambda(p)}\big)
, such that

\overline{G}\big(\overline{\lambda(p)}\big)\in B_{\delta_{1,t^{\ast}}+\delta_{2,\beta}}(\lambda(p))\subset K_{\kappa}(\lambda(p))\subset K_{r_{1}}(\lambda(p))\subset\mathcal{K}_{1},

(5.24)

as by Lemma
5.6
it holds
K_{r_{1}}(\lambda(p))\subset\mathcal{K}_{1}
.

6.

Necessary conditions for approximation:
We prove our main Theorem
5.4
by contradiction. To that purpose we assume that there exists a neural DDE
\Phi\in\textup{NDDE}^{k}_{\tau,\text{N},K}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
satisfying the assumptions of the theorem, for which the corresponding globally defined neural DDE is denoted by
\overline{\Phi}\in\overline{\textup{NDDE}}^{k}_{\tau,\text{N},K}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
with
\overline{\Phi}(x)=\Phi(x)
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
, which approximates the given map
\Psi
with accuracy
\varepsilon>0
. In particular this implies the component map
\Psi_{i}
is approximated by
\overline{\Phi}_{i}
with accuracy
\varepsilon
, i.e.,

\left\lVert\overline{\Phi}_{i}(x)-\Psi_{i}(x)\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\left\lVert\overline{\Phi}(x)-\Psi(x)\right\rVert_{\infty}<\varepsilon.

(5.25)

Inserting the non-degenerate local minimum
p\in\mathcal{X}
of
\Psi_{i}
in (
5.25
) and using the estimate (
5.21
), it follows

\displaystyle\left\lVert\overline{\Phi}_{i}(p)-\Psi_{i}(p)\right\rVert_{\infty}=\left\lVert\tilde{\lambda}_{i}(H(\lambda(p)))-\Psi_{i}(p)\right\rVert_{\infty}<\varepsilon,

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad

\displaystyle H(\lambda(p))\in\mathcal{Y}_{3}\coloneqq\tilde{\lambda}^{-1}_{i}\left((\Psi_{i}(p)-\varepsilon,\Psi_{i}(p)+\varepsilon)\right)\subset\mathbb{R}^{m},

(5.26)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\overline{H}\big(\overline{\lambda(p)}\big)\in\mathcal{Y}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau}}\coloneqq\tilde{\lambda}^{-1}_{i}\left((\Psi_{i}(p)-\varepsilon-\delta_{3,\tau},\Psi_{i}(p)+\varepsilon+\delta_{3,\tau})\right)\subset\mathbb{R}^{m},

(5.27)

with the open sets
\mathcal{Y}_{3}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau}}
defined in (
5.13
) and (
5.15
). For any point
y_{0}\in\partial\mathcal{K}_{0}
it holds by Lemma
5.6
(a) that
\Psi_{i}(y_{0})=\Psi_{i}(p)+r_{0}^{2}
. As we assume that
m=n
and
\text{rank}(W)=n
, Lemma
5.6
(b) additionally states that
\Psi_{i}(\lambda^{-1}(y_{1}))=\Psi_{i}(p)+r_{0}^{2}
for every
y_{1}\in\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
. It follows for
y_{1}\in\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
with the estimates (
5.21
) and (
5.25
) that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\overline{\Phi}_{i}(\lambda^{-1}(y_{1}))-\Psi_{i}(\lambda^{-1}(y_{1}))\right\rVert_{\infty}=\left\lVert\tilde{\lambda}_{i}(H(y_{1}))-\Psi_{i}(p)-r_{0}^{2}\right\rVert_{\infty}<\varepsilon,

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad

\displaystyle H(y_{1})\in\mathcal{Z}_{3}\coloneqq\tilde{\lambda}^{-1}_{i}\left((\Psi_{i}(p)+r_{0}^{2}-\varepsilon,\Psi_{i}(p)+r_{0}^{2}+\varepsilon)\right)\subset\mathbb{R}^{m},

(5.28)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\overline{H}(\bar{y}_{1})\in\mathcal{Z}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau}}\coloneqq\tilde{\lambda}^{-1}_{i}\left((\Psi_{i}(p)+r_{0}^{2}-\varepsilon-\delta_{3,\tau},\Psi_{i}(p)+r_{0}^{2}+\varepsilon+\delta_{3,\tau})\right)\subset\mathbb{R}^{m},

(5.29)

with the open sets
\mathcal{Z}_{3}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau}}
defined in (
5.14
) and (
5.16
). The necessary conditions for approximation (
5.26
)-(
5.29
) are visualized in Figure
5.6
6(a)
. As by assumption of Theorem
5.4
it holds
0<2\varepsilon<r_{0}^{2}
, it follows from Lemma
5.15
that there exists
\tau_{3}>0
, such that for all
\tau\in[0,\tau_{3}]
it holds

\mathcal{Y}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau}}\subset\mathcal{Y}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau_{3}}},\qquad\mathcal{Z}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau}}\subset\mathcal{Z}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau_{3}}},\qquad\mathcal{Y}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau}}=\varnothing

and

\big\lVert\tilde{\lambda}_{i}(y_{3})-\tilde{\lambda}_{i}(z_{3})\big\lVert_{\infty}\geq r_{0}^{2}-2\varepsilon-2\delta_{3,\tau_{3}}\eqqcolon\delta^{\ast}>0

for all
y_{3}\in\mathcal{Y}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau}}
and
z_{3}\in\mathcal{Z}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau}}
.

To also characterize the solution map of the initial value problem (
5.7
), which generates all special solutions of the DDE (
5.3
), over the time interval
[t^{\ast},T]
, the solution map
\overline{I}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
is defined in (
5.10
). By definition, it holds
\overline{H}=\overline{I}\circ\overline{G}
, where
\overline{G}
and
\overline{H}
are the solution maps of the ODE (
5.7
) over the time intervals
[0,t^{\ast}]
and
[0,T]
as defined in (
5.8
) and (
5.9
).

By Lemma
5.9
, the map
\overline{I}:\mathcal{K}_{2,\delta_{2,\beta}}\rightarrow\overline{I}(\mathcal{K}_{2,\delta_{2,\beta}})
is a
C^{k}
-diffeomorphism. Applying the inverse
\overline{I}^{-1}
to the estimates (
5.27
) and (
5.29
) results in the following necessary conditions:

\displaystyle\overline{I}^{-1}\big(\overline{H}\big(\overline{\lambda(p)}\big)\big)=\overline{G}\big(\overline{\lambda(p)}\big)

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{Y}_{2}\coloneqq\overline{I}^{-1}(\mathcal{Y}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau_{3}}})\subset\mathcal{H}_{\Psi_{i}(p)+\varepsilon+\delta_{3,\tau_{3}}}^{<},

(5.30)

\displaystyle\overline{I}^{-1}(\overline{H}(\bar{y}_{1}))=\overline{G}(\bar{y}_{1})

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{Z}_{2}\coloneqq\overline{I}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau_{3}}})\subset\mathcal{H}_{\Psi_{i}(p)+r_{0}^{2}-\varepsilon-\delta_{3,\tau_{3}}}^{>},

(5.31)

where
\bar{y}_{1}
is the initial condition of the corresponding special solution of
y(0,c_{y_{1}})
with
y_{1}\in\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
. Hereby
\mathcal{H}_{z}^{<}
and
\mathcal{H}_{z}^{>}
are by Lemma
5.13
the unbounded open components of
\mathbb{R}^{m}\setminus\mathcal{H}_{z}
, where

\mathcal{H}_{z}\coloneqq\overline{I}^{-1}(\tilde{\lambda}_{i}^{-1}(z))

is for every
z\in\mathbb{R}
a closed
C^{k}
-hypersurface. If
y\in\mathcal{H}_{z}^{<}
, then
\tilde{\lambda}_{i}(\overline{I}(y))<z
and if
y\in\mathcal{H}_{z}^{>}
, then
\tilde{\lambda}_{i}(\overline{I}(y))>z
. The closure of the open sets
\mathcal{H}_{z}^{<}
and
\mathcal{H}_{z}^{>}
is denoted by
\mathcal{H}_{z}^{\leq}\coloneqq\mathcal{H}_{z}^{<}\cup\mathcal{H}_{z}
and
\mathcal{H}_{z}^{\geq}\coloneqq\mathcal{H}_{z}^{>}\cup\mathcal{H}_{z}
respectively. The necessary conditions (
5.30
) and (
5.31
) are visualized in Figure
5.6
6(b)
.

(a)

Necessary conditions (
5.26
) and (
5.28
) for the map
H
and necessary conditions (
5.27
) and (
5.29
) for the map
\overline{H}
at time
t=T
.

.

(b)

Necessary conditions (
5.30
) and (
5.31
) for the map
\overline{G}
at time
t=t^{\ast}
, obtained after applying the map
\overline{I}^{-1}
to the conditions (
5.27
) and (
5.29
).

Figure 5.6
:
Necessary conditions for approximation implied by the estimate (
5.25
) for the local minimum
p\in\mathcal{X}
and all points of the boundary
y_{0}\in\partial\mathcal{K}_{0}
or
y_{1}\in\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
respectively.

By Theorem
4.11
, the initial value problem (
5.7
) has a globally defined and in the second variable Lipschitz continuous vector field with Lipschitz constant
Ke
. By Theorem
4.3
(b), which applies also for ODEs, as they are DDEs with zero delay, it holds

\displaystyle\left\lVert\overline{I}(y_{2})-\overline{I}(z_{2})\right\rVert_{\infty}=

\displaystyle\left\lVert y(t^{\ast},y_{2})(T)-y(t^{\ast},z_{2})(T)\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left\lVert y_{2}-z_{2}\right\rVert_{\infty}e^{Ke(T-t^{\ast})}\leq\left\lVert y_{2}-z_{2}\right\rVert_{\infty}e^{KeT}

(5.32)

for all
y_{2},z_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

7.

Deriving a contradiction:
Let the approximation assumption (
5.25
) hold and assume

0<\kappa\leq r_{1},\quad\beta=\beta_{\kappa}\coloneqq\ln\left(\frac{2C_{2}}{\kappa}\right)\geq 0\quad\text{and}\quad 0\leq\tau<\tau_{0}\coloneqq\min\left\{\tau_{1,\beta,\kappa},\frac{1}{Ke},\frac{T}{\beta},\tau_{3}\right\},

(5.33)

which are the assumptions (
5.22
) combined with
\tau\in[0,\tau_{3}]
to guarantee that the sets
\mathcal{Y}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau_{3}}}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{3,\delta_{3,\tau_{3}}}
do not intersect. By Lemma
5.15
, it holds under these assumptions that

\big\lVert\tilde{\lambda}_{i}(\overline{I}(y_{2}))-\tilde{\lambda}_{i}(\overline{I}(z_{2}))\big\lVert_{\infty}\geq\delta^{\ast}>0

(5.34)

for all
y_{2}\in\mathcal{H}^{\leq}_{\Psi_{i}(p)+\varepsilon+\delta_{3,\tau_{3}}}
and
z_{2}\in\mathcal{H}^{\geq}_{\Psi_{i}(p)+r_{0}^{2}-\varepsilon-\delta_{3,\tau_{3}}}
.

By Lemma
5.14
for
z=\Psi_{i}(p)+\varepsilon+\delta_{3,\tau_{3}}
, there exists
y_{1}^{\ast}\in\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
with
y_{1}^{\ast}\in\mathcal{H}^{\leq}_{\Psi_{i}(p)+\varepsilon+\delta_{3,\tau_{3}}}
. The assumption of Lemma
5.14
that there exists
y_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}
with
y_{1}\in\mathcal{H}^{<}_{\Psi_{i}(p)+\varepsilon+\delta_{3,\tau_{3}}}
is fulfilled as by (
5.24
) it holds
\overline{G}(\overline{\lambda(p)})\in\mathcal{K}_{1}
and by (
5.30
) it holds
\overline{G}(\overline{\lambda(p)})\in\mathcal{H}^{<}_{\Psi_{i}(p)+\varepsilon+\delta_{3,\tau_{3}}}
. The points
y_{1}^{\ast}\in\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
and
\overline{G}(\overline{\lambda(p)})\in\mathcal{K}_{1}
are visualized in Figure
5.7
.

Now fix
\kappa=\min\left\{\frac{\delta^{\ast}}{\tilde{w}e^{KeT}},r_{1}\right\}>0
, where
\tilde{w}
is the fixed constant with
\big\lVert\widetilde{W}\big\lVert_{\infty}\leq\tilde{w}
. As in the setting of Theorem
5.4
, the weight matrices
W,\widetilde{W}
are bounded in the sup-norm by
w,\tilde{w}
and the constants
A
,
r_{0}
,
\varepsilon
and the final time
T
are fixed, the constant
\kappa
, and hence also
\beta_{\kappa}
and
\tau_{0}
, depend continuously on the Lipschitz constant
K
of the neural DDE and are independent of the explicit choice of the vector field of the neural DDE. The function
\tau_{0}\in C^{0}((0,\infty),[0,T])
is continuous with
K\tau_{0}(K)e<1
for
K\in(0,\infty)
.

In the following, we use the Lipschitz continuity of the map
\tilde{\lambda}_{i}\circ\overline{I}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
at the considered point
y_{1}^{\ast}\in\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
with
y_{1}^{\ast}\in\mathcal{H}^{\leq}_{\Psi_{i}(p)+\varepsilon+\delta_{3,\tau_{3}}}
. We can estimate for all
y_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
with
\left\lVert y_{1}^{\ast}-y_{1}\right\rVert_{\infty}<\kappa
that

\displaystyle\big\lVert\tilde{\lambda}_{i}(\overline{I}(y_{1}^{\ast}))-\tilde{\lambda}_{i}(\overline{I}(y_{1}))\big\lVert_{\infty}\leq\;

\displaystyle\tilde{w}\left\lVert\overline{I}(y_{1}^{\ast})-\overline{I}(y_{1})\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle\tilde{w}e^{KeT}\left\lVert y_{1}^{\ast}-y_{1}\right\rVert_{\infty}<\tilde{w}e^{KeT}\kappa\leq\delta^{\ast},

(5.35)

where we first used the affine linear structure of the map
\tilde{\lambda}
and then the estimate (
5.32
) and the definition of
\kappa
.

Now we consider the points
y_{1}^{\ast}
and
\overline{G}(\bar{y}_{1}^{\ast})
, for which it holds by the estimate (
5.23
),

\left\lVert y_{1}^{\ast}-\overline{G}(\bar{y}_{1}^{\ast})\right\rVert_{\infty}<\kappa,

hence
\overline{G}(\bar{y}_{1}^{\ast})\in B_{\kappa}(\bar{y}_{1}^{\ast})
, as visualized in Figure
5.7
. From this estimate, the inequality (
5.35
) implies that

\big\lVert\tilde{\lambda}_{i}(\overline{I}(y_{1}^{\ast}))-\tilde{\lambda}_{i}(\overline{I}(G(\bar{y}_{1}^{\ast})))\big\lVert_{\infty}<\delta^{\ast}.

(5.36)

On the other side, it is by inclusion (
5.31
) a necessary condition for approximation that
\overline{G}(\bar{y}_{1}^{\ast})\in\mathcal{H}^{\geq}_{\Psi_{i}(p)+r_{0}^{2}-\varepsilon-\delta_{3,\tau_{3}}}
as
y_{1}^{\ast}\in\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
, which is also visualized in Figure
5.7
. As a consequence, for
y_{2}=y_{1}^{\ast}
and
z_{2}=\overline{G}(\bar{y}_{1}^{\ast})
the estimate (
5.34
) implies that

\big\lVert\tilde{\lambda}_{i}(\overline{I}(y_{1}^{\ast}))-\tilde{\lambda}_{i}(\overline{I}(\overline{G}(\bar{y}_{1}^{\ast})))\big\lVert_{\infty}\geq\delta^{\ast},

which contradicts the continuity estimate (
5.36
). The contradiction is visualized in Figure
5.7
as follows: for an approximation it is necessary, that
\overline{G}(\bar{y}_{1}^{\ast})\in\mathcal{Z}_{2}
, but by continuity it holds
\overline{G}(\bar{y}_{1}^{\ast})\in B_{\kappa}(\bar{y}_{1}^{\ast})
. For every
\tau\in[0,\tau_{0}(K))
, the constant
\kappa
is sufficiently small such that
\mathcal{Z}_{2}\cap B_{\kappa}(\bar{y}_{1}^{\ast})=\varnothing
and under the given assumptions no approximation of
\Psi
by a neural DDE
\overline{\Phi}
is possible with accuracy
\varepsilon
.

Figure 5.7
:
Visualization of the contradiction derived in point 7.) of the proof of Theorem
5.4
at time
t=t^{\ast}
. On the one hand side, it is by (
5.31
) a necessary condition for approximation that
\overline{G}(\bar{y}_{1}^{\ast})\in\mathcal{Z}_{2}
. On the other side, for
\tau\in[0,\tau_{0}(K))
, the constant
\kappa
is sufficiently small, such that the open ball around the considered point
y_{1}^{\ast}\in\partial\mathcal{K}_{1}
with radius
\kappa
does not intersect with
\mathcal{Z}_{2}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{Z}_{2}\cap B_{\kappa}(\bar{y}_{1}^{\ast})=\varnothing
. As this is a contradiction to
\overline{G}(\bar{y}_{1}^{\ast})\in\mathcal{Z}_{2}
, no approximation of the map
\Psi
by any considered neural DDE is possible.

Hence, for
\tau\in[0,\tau_{0}(K))
, every neural DDE
\overline{\Phi}\in\overline{\textup{NDDE}}_{\tau,\textup{N},K}^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
,
k\geq 1
, with underlying vector field
F:\Omega_{t}\times\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
with global Lipschitz constant
K
in its second variable on
\Omega_{t}\times\mathcal{C}
and
\left\lVert F(t,0)\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq A
for all
t\in[0,T]
cannot approximate the map
\Psi
with accuracy
\varepsilon
. With the argumentation of point 3.), the same result follows for every neural DDE
\Phi\in\textup{NDDE}_{\tau,\textup{N},K}^{k}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
, which satisfies the assumptions of the theorem.
∎

6
Conclusion and Outlook

In this work, we studied the approximation capability of neural DDEs depending on their memory capacity, i.e., depending on the product
K\tau
of the Lipschitz constant and the delay of the vector field. Neural DDEs can be interpreted as an infinite-depth limit of densely connected residual neural networks (DenseResNets), which allow for feed-forward shortcut connections between all layers. DenseResNets unify the general idea of existing neural network architectures with memory terms and inter-layer connections. Studying the continuous-time neural network allows the usage of well-developed theory of delay differential equations. By an Euler discretization of the considered neural DDEs, results for discrete-time neural networks such as DenseResNets can be established.

Of significant interest are non-augmented architectures where the dimension of the DDE is not larger than the input or output dimension. Non-augmented MLPs, ResNets, and neural ODEs show a restricted approximation capability
[
19
,
28
,
32
,
41
]
. As neural ODEs are neural DDEs with delay
\tau=0
, it follows that if
K\tau=0
, neural DDEs cannot have the universal approximation property. In Theorem
3.13
, we have shown that if the memory capacity
K\tau
is sufficiently small, neural DDEs still lack the universal approximation property. The infinite-dimensional phase space of DDEs with
\tau>0
is not sufficient to approximate every element of the function space
C^{j}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}^{q})
,
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
open, for every
j\geq 0
. The proof uses a scalar function
\Psi_{i}
with a non-degenerate local extreme point, i.e., locally, the function
\Psi_{i}
has a minimum or maximum. The topological constraints of unique solution curves in the finite-dimensional state space of ODEs prevent scalar neural ODEs from approximating local extreme points. For
K\tau
sufficiently small, this property carries over to neural DDEs. It is important to work with non-degenerate local extreme points, as it is possible that scalar neural ODEs and neural DDEs approximate saddle points.

As numerically observed for many feed-forward neural network architectures with additional inter-layer connections, the approximation capability increases with the memory capacity
[
26
,
47
,
53
,
54
]
. If the memory capacity
K\tau
is sufficiently large, we have shown in Theorem
3.9
that neural DDEs have the universal embedding property with respect to the space of Lipschitz continuous functions. We were able to show an exact representation of any Lipschitz continuous function, as the vector field of the DDE is a parameter of the network itself. If the vector field has an explicit and rich enough parameterization, parameterized neural DDEs with sufficient memory capacity have the universal approximation property. The parameter region where universal embedding is possible can be increased for augmented architectures with
m\geq p+q
, as shown in Theorem
3.14
. The proof works similarly to neural ODEs and does not use any delayed terms. Overall, we specify for large parts of the three-dimensional parameter space
(K,\tau,m)
of Lipschitz constant, delay, and dimension of the vector field, where universal embedding and no universal approximation are possible. Nevertheless, the behavior near critical parameter values remains for future work. In that way, we provide, in the context of continuous-time models, a fundamental understanding of how the memory capacity influences the universal approximation property.

It is left for future work to transfer the established results to discrete-time architectures. In Section
2.1
, we stated the necessary assumptions for the discretization of a neural DDE to become a DenseResNet. For specific discrete-time architectures, the concrete parameterized form of the neural DDE needs to be specified. Furthermore, the discretization error between neural DDEs and DenseResNets needs to be studied, as done for neural ODEs and ResNets in
[
48
]
.

Further interesting research areas are the implications that a large or a small memory capacity has on the training process or the generalization properties of neural DDEs and DenseResNets. In the context of learning algorithms, additional memory in a neural network can mitigate the vanishing gradient problem and influence the convergence behavior. Regarding generalization properties, it is important to analyze whether the increasing expressivity due to a larger memory capacity promotes overfitting.
